PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

"Mir dear friends," said the nervous clergy- man, "Jacob sold his birthright for a pot of mes- sage." "An," said the old lady, illustrating moral depravity, "I have seen little children that could neither walk nor talk, run about the streets blaspheming." BANKER (finding a burglar in his office) : "Thieves ! Help! Help!" Burglar: "You'd better stop that row, man, else I'll tell everybody I found nothing in your safe." ANDERSON: My memory is getting weaker and weaker every day.' Biggs: "I can give you a remedy." " What is it 1" " Lend me a sovereign."

A CERTAIN debating society is discussing the question as to which is the angrier—the husband who goes home and finds that dinner is not ready, or the wife who has dinner ready and whose hus- band does not come home. It is believed that the debate will end in a draw.

LT,CoCK'S CORN EXTRACTOR.

" CAPITAL " CHAT

Mr. Keir Hardie has again explained his defeat at South-West Ham, and he is evidently sore about it, but anyone with eyes to see could detect that it was coming more than a year ago, and he was told so. The fact is that he was not, from any point of view, a satisfactory member. His attention was irregular, and for whole weeks he would be absent. away lecturing, and doing his literary work in railway carriages, whilst important divisions were taken or great matters were being discussed. No constituents were likely to pardon these peculi- arities when they were associated with wildness In other ways. He now threatens to stop his paper unless it is better supported. The "Labour Leader" is not alone in its difficuties. Every organ of the Socialists may be said to be in a chronic state of bankruptcy. I say this the more cot. fidently be- cause I have been behind the scenes and know that Socialist journalism is a kind of prison discipline, and half its venom is due to want of pay on the part of the writers.

Lord Rosebery's friends in London. who knew what an enormous amount of work he did in con- nection with the London Council, to the permanent injury of his health—for his insomnia began during the period of greatest stress—are very indignant with the description of him in the "Spectator" as "a butterfly Premier." He was anything but that, and noon° who knows him intimately would dream of so depicting him. But there is no doubt that the habit of joking and poking fun at opponents in after dinner speeches, and refraining from self- glorification does create on impression of levity and skimmingness, though it is seldom borne out by facts. In Lord Rosebery's case it certainly was not. He worked about fifteen hours a day when he was chief of the County Council, and he made others , work also.

Mr. John Morley, freed from the cares of office, , will now complete his half-finished biography of Chatham, long promised in the " Twelve English Statesmen" series. Be has another historical work in contemplation—nothing less than a history of Ireland during the establishment of the union. Be has studied most carefully a large collection of State papers, dating from 1795 to 1805, and consist- ing of letters from informers, petitions of con- demned prisoners of the '9B rebellion, and private communications from Irish magistrates and Gov- ernment officials. The new work will be founded on this material.

Now the Geographical Congress is over, a slight but interesting Franco-German incident that arose in connection with it may be mentioned. A river steamer which happened to be named Bismarck had been chartered to take the company down to the Geographical Cub dinner at Greenwich. It was made known on behalf of the French delegates,that if a boat called the Bismarck must carry the party, they must remain absent. Of course another steamer was obtained, the Duke of Cambridge be- ing the harmless name of it. "The Duke "offends nobody. Such childish forms does patriotism some- times take. On the very evening of its opening by the Duke of York, the Geographical Congress fur- nished another homily on the niceties of Eurcpean jealousy. Who was to acknowledge the Duke's wel- come on the part of the foreign delegates? If one were taken the other must be lef t,or if one were first the other must be second. But the difficulty was obviated by a happy notion. Ex-Chief Justice Daly, an American, representing a nation standing by it- self, moreover the oldest president of any geograp- hical society in Congress, was asked to acknow- ledge the welcome.

It is expected that before the year is out the Government will appoint a Poet Laureate. The difficulty of the late Government wa that the only name they could select was not acceptable. Lord Salisbury is not under any delicncy of this kind. and I have reason for saying that he will suggest a name to Her Majesty at en early date. He will not be a Tennyson or a Swinburne. but no one will be able to say of him that he is a 'prentice hand at " the rhyming trade."

The princely athlete known over here sometimes as "Mr. Ranjitsinhji," sometimes more familiarly ' as "Smith," is it seems,not content with the pride of place he has won In the hierarchy of cricket ; MS ambition soars to a seat in the House. It is not impossible that be may yet take a higher seat. sir. I.tanjitsinhji is,when he is at home, a Rnjput Prince of quite unmixed descent. Until he was sixteen or ,so he was the acknowledged heir to the purple at Jaranagar, says the "Realm." SCRIP family com- plications ensued, and Mr. Ranjitsinhji's adoptive father,the Jam of Jamnagar,adopted somebody else. The Prince came to England, and at Cambridge he developed into a first-class cricketer. Now,counter complications have arisen, and he may be called upon to go back and be Jam of Jamnagar, after all.

Mr. Chamberlain's announcement that the Liberal Unionist members of Parliament would henceforth receive the usual parliamentary circulars and whips not from himself, but from Mr. Balfour, has given rise to more talk in political centres than it really deserves. It by no means implies the absorption of he Liberal Unionist in the Conservative party,since all the registration machinery of the party outside the House remains separate and distinct. Among the moderate Liberals, as well as the Radicals, the notion is very prevalent that the di,eppenrance of the separate organization of the Liberal Unionists inside the Liouse was equivalent to fusion, hut any observant Liberal must have been very well aware that the relations of the two parties rpte not N et rip. for compete amalgamation, Whether the Unionist are ever likely to be again split up into their two original elements is a dllf,rent question, which must be left to time to solve ; but there is scarcely a Conservative or a Liberal to be found who does not believe that the day is not very remot- when the Liberal Unionist will cease to exist as a sub- stantial and recognisable party,

Mr. Michael Datitt was lecturing in Adelaide, the metropolis of South Australia. when the first inti- mation of an immediate General Election was re- ceived by cable. He imtnncliately telegraphed tn Mr. Justin McCarthy, who (-OW in reply :—"Need urgent: elections irtmediate." When the mail which has lust arrived in London was leaving Australia on June 26, Nfr. Dayitt's future movements were uncertain, as he thought he might he recalled to Ireland at any moment. In reply to the question, "Would you stand for a constittioney if yem reached home in time ?" Mr. Davit said, "My own views are opposed to my re-entry into the Nouse of Cm- mona. Short, as my parliamentary career has been I have had more than enough of it,as a residence in London for half the year entails ton big a sacrifice for a poor man from Ireland. However, if Mr. Mc- Carthy and the Irish party insist unon my re-entry, then, as a matter ot duty,l would find it extremely difficult to refuse."

The turnover at the elections has made it very awkward relative to the prosecution of Jahez Balfour. He must now be prosecuted by a Conser- vative law official. though the case was being care- fully got up by Sir Frank Lockwood at the time of the defeat of the late Governm.nt. Indeed, it looked stone time as if the present Government were delaying its legal appointments in order not to disturb the arrangements made for this trial. hut now the cases will not -!orrie on until the end of October any compromise of that kind would seem to be less likely.

It has been pleasint to note the sweet and grace- iul things said about the old man who spoke at Chester on Tuesday. The sunset of Mr. Gladstone's life does not give him mystical lore, but it gives tints and tones that harmonise with its past and foreshadow something like a canonization. Even the younger Conservatives have restrained their cynicism, and for once seem to have been able to fervently admire one of his orations. Ido not wish to analyse this change too curiously, hut it is one to be noted and enjoyed. It is the mood that will last and the building of altars has now tstken the place of the flinging of stones.

The great wheel at Earl's Court stands up over the bouses so prominently that it can be seen from many of the hills south of London,and it is a pretty sight when slowly revolving on a cle-ar night in its circle of lights. Seen from Richmond it is like a small Catherine wheel that has somewhat k.pent its force and bee been spinning from some lofty structure. The view from the wiles' of London it- self depends upon so many conditions of atmos- phere that it is seldom clear, but it.is always effec- tive, and in the blaze of early night it is intensely picturesque—ft kind of brok.n milky war.

Potatoes do not stain the hands if peeled while quite dry, and not thrown into water till afterwards. When mixing mustard always put some salt in it, as it keeps it from beccming dis- coloured and from turning sour. A neat little device for preventing buttons from com- ing off is the following : Make a hole in the kid large enough for the eye of each button, then take a round shoe string and pass it through every button, fastening the string at each end. In this way the buttons will be kept in place as long as the shoe lasts. Coloured prints and muslins, likely to fade when washed, can be " set" with salt and water, and their pristine brightness never afterwards lost, no matter how hard the washing. This is especially true of blue, and mourning prints of white figures on a black ground so treated will be black to the end of the chapter. Crime is more common in single life than in married. In the former thirty-three in every one hundred thousand are guilty, while only eleven married men of the same number have gravely broken the laws. Sir Benjamm Richardson thinks that the normal period of human life is about one hundred and ten years and that seven out of ten people ought to live to that age if they took proper care of themselves. Mrs. Frances Hodgson Burnett has extraordinary notions about bringing up children. Hers have never been allowed to be corrected since they were born. Rowever much they may have outraged the traditions of good behaviour, sense of shame and the force of ex- ample were the only remedies permitted. It was RS much as a governess's or a servant's place was worth to forget this rule. - It would appear that Count Tolstoi has made a con- vert not merely to his views, but to his self-denying practice, in the person of a countess who has in her time been a lady-in-waiting at the Court of Berlin. Address- ing a gathering of working women in Copenhagen, the other day, she announced that she intended to sell her mansion near the Danish capital and devote the proceeds to the poor. She had live I, she said, in the palace of an Emperor and in the halts of fishermen, and she had be- come convinced that the poor man is generally happier than the millionaire.

Bleeding from the Nose. Bleeding from the nose is seldQm serious, and maV generally be controlled by the application of cold water. The patient should be kept upright,with his head thrown back and his hands raised above his head,with a sponge or some cotton wool over his nose to receive the blood ; but he should on no account be allowed to stand with his head bent down over a basin, which encourages the bleeding.

A Home-Made Cement.

A very convenient cement can be made out of onion juice by any one who wishes to use it. A good-sized Spanish onion, after being boiled for a short time, will yield, on being pressed, quite a large quantity of very adhesive fluid. This is used extensively in various trades for pasting paper on to tin, or zinc, or even glass, and the tenacity with which it holds would surprise any one on making the first attempt. It is a cheap and good mucilage, and answers as well as the more costly cements.

and had made out clearly that the crew of the pirate numbered over a hundred men—that they were armed to the teeth—and that the long brass gun was ready for action. His feet had just touched the deck on his return when a wreath of smoke was seen to curl up from the brig's deck, and in a moment more a round shot struck the water under the ship's quarter. Capt. Harlow called his men aft and addressed 'diem. He pointed to the pirate and bade them temember what must be their fate if they were overcome. Would they die like dogs, or would they die like men whom God had made brave and strong for the defence of life and right? There was but one response to the commander's appeal. It was plain to be seen that not a few of the crew were pale and agitated, and that their lips and their limbs quivered; but Sir Jasper knew, when he looked upon them, that these very men were those who might fight with the most fiery determination when the crack of musketry and the slash of steel had awakened the demon of battle within them.

Arthur Rainsford, who had buckled on his sword and secured his own double-barrelled jungle pistols —a pair that had belonged to his father—to his belt, stood by the taffrail watthing the brig, when he felt a hand upon his shoulder. He turned, and behold Sir Jasper Wallace who signified that he desired to speak with him in private. Arthur followed him to the rail of the main rigging, where they stopped. There was a sad and solemn look upon the old soldier's face, and his lip quivered when he spoke. "Mr. Rainsford, I have looked carefully into your face, and I think you are a brave, true man." "I trust I may prove a true man, sir, if not a brave one," returned Arthur, modestly. "And," pursued the General, with a change of tone which would seem to indicate that he was making a digression from tho main subject in view, "the more I see of you the more I am reminded of a brave and true man who was once my friend. Have you ever been in India ?" "I was born there, sir." "Born in India! Then it may be possible, after all, that you bear some relationship to Tom Rains- ford ?"

"My father, sir, was Colonel Tom Rainsford, who fell at Hajepoor." Sir Jasper reached forth and grasped the youth's hand quickly and eagerly. "Thank God that I have a son of Tom Rainsford to stand by me in this hour !" he ejaculated, fer- vently. "Your father served under me, Arthur, .snd was one of the truest and bravest among the true and the brave. Through many a field of blood Lave we striven together. Poor Tom! I saw him fall, and the dusky dead were piled around him in beeps." A moment they held each other by the hand in silence. At length the solemn look returned to Sir Jasper's face, and with a corresponding change in his manner he said :

"If we outlive this day we will talk of those bygone times • but there is another duty now before us. We know not what en hour may bring forth. For myself I care not. I have faced mortal peril too often to quail at this which now threatens. But lam not alone exposed. There is one dearer to me than life—made dear because she leans upon and trusts me—who must partake of the danger. Arthur Rainsford, you have looked upon Mary Wallace and you know what must be her fate if—"

Arthur put forth his hand as though to ward off a serpent. "Hush 1" he exclaimed. "God will not permit it!"

"Alas! we know not what present evil Heaven may suffer to fall upon us. But enough of that. I am not so strong as I once was, and I may fall in the battle. If 1 am stricken down and you shall survive will you look to her? Will you guard her with your life? Will you make her salvation your sole aim ?"

"As God heareth me, I will I" answered the youth, slowly and solemnly. "I see in your face and in your frame strength, and fire and zeal," pursued the old soldier ; "and I shall feel better to know that you are sworn to the protection of that priceless jewel." " You have my word," answered Arthur, man- fully, "and let the end decide how I keep the faith. But you will not fall, Sir Jasper." "If," went on the knight, without heeding the youth's last remark, "I should be taken away and the worst should come, I do not think they would capture my precious treasure alive. 0, Arthur Raiusford, help me to save her !"

Our hero knew but too well the fearful picture which the old man had conjured up—the picture of self-destruction as the last resort of the maiden —and he felt that it was by no means far-fetched. The thought struck a momentary horror to his soul, which was followed by new strength of purpose._ . . .

"Sir Jasper," lie said, putting forth his hand once more, "I understand you. Trust me while I live."

As they turned towards the poop they met Mary coming out from the cabin, and a new light gleamed in her eye as she saw who bore her guar- dian company. She seemed to comprehend on th.e instant that she had a new champion, and the glow of satisfaction that suffused her face told plainly that the discovery was not an unwelcome one.

"My dear child." said Sir Jasper, taking her hand—he spoke lightly and cheerfully, as though he would lift her out from the gloom as far as possible—" I have found a prize. This is the son of that Tom Rainsford of whom you have so often heard me speak, and I am sure we could not have found a better friend in this emergency." Mary gave her hand to Arthur, and with a sweet smile she said:

- ..... "Your presence, Mr. Rainsford, I hold as a blessing indeed." "If I can be of service to you in this hour, dear lady, I shall account the Ileed as the richest bless- ing of my life. Let us hope for the best." The maiden did not cast down her eyes, nor did she shrink from the earnest, devoted gaze of the youth vhose speech had been so warm and ardent. With the truth and candour of a soul that dared acknowledge its own best impulses, she returned a grateful acknowledgement and freely accepted the proffered championship. A brief pause, and then she said:

"I think I know the danger; and I am prepared to meet it. If you have a thought of this poor self of mine let it be not such as to harass or perplex you. I will hold myself as free from the danger as possible. "You will go below, Mary. Trust not your- self in the cabin again. It is too much exposed there."

She was upon the point of replying when an- other report from the pirate's gun broke the air, and with it came the crashing of an iron ball directly through the cabin. This settled the ques- tion of the place of her retreat, and she promised that she would go into the ward-room when she left the deck. But she could not go yet. She could not bear the suspense of dark uncertainty while she could witness the proceedings without mortal danger. She had not quailed at the crash- ing of the shot through the bulwarks of the cabin, and she would not retire while she could show a face of calm decision to her friends. Her guardian might have insisted upon her going below at once; but he markell the effect which her presence had upon the sailors, and when he remembered the hidden springs which operate upon the human heart, he understood that the youth and the beauty of the English maiden might find a responsive strength of good-will in those British bosoms which nothing else could have awakened. So he left her standing by one of the guns, at which a number of the sailors were stationed, While he went to confer with the captain. (To be continued).

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DZAREST NELLIE,

The distinctive feature of the season's fashions, aside from the elaborated sleeves, and wide, plain skirts, is the craze for lace and all sorts and kinds of showy ornamentation in jetted and spangled trimmings of brilliant colourings, and Persian bands of numerous soft, subdued tints woven together with charming effect. Added to these is the univer- sal use of white and cream tints on dark as well as light dresses. There are rows of fine tucks alternated with narrow insertion and edged with lace insertion set in diagonally, lengthwise, and straight across without tucks or edging, and inch. wide horizontal tucks with insertion between and edge gathered in each tuck. A new pattern for a jacket, which is even better than a reefer for sea- side wear, is called a "blouse jacket," has a straight front and back, and is made in panels, which are braided to the shoulders. The two fronts form one panel, the sides two other panels, and the back another. The sleeves are full above and made with a long cuff that extends half over the wrist.

Cambric is an excellent material for blouses. A pretty one can be made in spotted cambric trimmed with lace insertion. It has a plain close-fitting lining foundation. The front is trimmed with a V-shape yoke and completed with a full overpart gathered to meet it under a band of insertion, and it is fastened invisibly with hooks in the centre. The back is gathered at the neck and waist over the plain foundation. The high collar is trimmed with a row of insertion, and a belt of the material neatens the waist, and is fastened with a buckle in front. The full sleeves are gathered over a plain foundation, and are finished at the wrist with a deep cuff trimmed with, two rows of insertion. Others, again, are made of silk, and these look exceedingly handsome. For instance, there was one made of shot green and chine silk. The sleeves were puffed to the elbow, and piped with narrow cream Valenciennes lace.

As for summer gowns I think I never saw prettier costumes than at Henley the other day. Those I liked most were perfectly simple, and made either of tambour muslin or of the new grass lawn, with coloured satin neck and waist bands. I cannot decide which was the cooler or daintier, the lawn or muslin. A really charming gown, was of palest green muslin, with a large fichu of white lawn and beurre Valenciennes lace and insertion. This was made to cover the whole back and front of the bodice, which was shaped with " defined " shoulders' a style that I believe will be firmly established by the winter. The neck and waist bands were of white satin, and the hat of white chip, trimmed with white wings and bows, and a profusion of pale green tulle arranged in a scarf and choux.

The new skirts and coats are made of fine faced cloth, principally in tan or stone colour, The skirts are very full, and there is no attempt to tie back or make them set plain in front. The coats lre a little shorter than they have been of late, and are ornamented in front with double sets of cloth or braid tabs with three buttons on each, and are cut low enough in the neck to admit of a smart vest or shirt front and a neck-band, and worn with washing cuffs and collars edged with lace. This muslin is sold by the yard in the form of tabs.

Satin or velvet makes up admirably in those stylish short capes. Black is undoubtedly the most useful colour, and may be trimmed with a champagne-tinted Brussels appliqué, or a beurre- coloured lace of the guipure stamp, combined with jet and sequins. Three and a-half yards of satin will make a wrap of this sort, and, if black gowns are much worn, the lace should be light in colour, and the open-patterned jet may be laid over narrow ribbon, in the light tint of the lace. The body portion of the zouave requires to be stiff and firm, and for this reason should be lined with linen, and again with sarcenet. The satin collar, with the large bow at the back, is part of the wrap, and keeps the upper portion neat and firm. The sleeves are simply lined with sarcenet, as the dress sleeves support them sufficiently, and the gathers make them stand out well, A short cascade of lace which falls from the bust-strap does not enlarge the figure at all, but gives a dainty effect to the front of the zouave. Some pretty hats are to be seen made of black tulle. A very youthful-looking one was made of drawn black tulle, the brim being edged with cream guipure. The wired bows are of white lace, and the trimming consists of masses of cornflowers and foliage, with large spear-shaped grass' and two blue and green shot wings. Choux of blue and green tulle, the tints of cornflower and leaf, may be substituted for the blossoms. Sailors' seem to maintain their perennial popularity. This is a

very pretty -panama sailor, .. a simple dark-blue ribbon as trimming, or quills and wings might be used. Your loving JENNIE.

At a fireworks display in Preston Palk, Brighton, en Monday night, a mortar burst and its fragments were scattered in all directions. Sixteen persons were in- jured, oniii of whom, a boy of tourteen, died yeate.day. The G.ruisn Emperor, who is the Queen's guest at Cowes, MI TllebtlaY paid a vi,t to his battleship Woerth, and in an address to the men—yesterday being the 25a) anniversary of the famous engagement between the French and German armies—advised them to emulate, should occasion require,their countrymen who took part in the battle after which the vessel was named.

Woman's Greatest Charm.

I am quite sute—Write-siiid-iniaisin the "Ladies Home Journal," that men regard " sweet simplicity " as the greatest charm in women, and especially in girls. This does not mean simplicity in the simpering sense, but an ab.ence of that affected air of boldness and mannishness which has lately been assumed by too many really lovable girls. Then, too, sincerity in ex- pression is one ot the characteristics that charm men. To be sincere and candid the girl in society need never be abrupt nor self-assertive.

A Graceful Carriage. There is a great charm in a graceful walk, expressing as it does the harmony of perfect balance. It is, how- ever, a charm that is rarely seen. People scuttle, patter, sniblP, shuffle, hurry, or stride along, but it is only here and there we see how well they walk. Owing to the lack of training in physical exercises, women move even less gracefully than men. To walk gracefully is to put the foot down prettily. Don't believe it when you are told to put the toe first. The foot should be so lightly poised un the ankle that when the lift from the hip is made in taking a step, the toe naturally swings toe down, so that the forward part of the foot touches the ground first. This is different from stiffly pointing the toe down and trying to walk that way. Put the feet down so that the heels would keep pretty closely on an imaginary line,the toes always falling a little outside of the line. The full weight of the body should be on the foot that is on the ground. One ought to be able to balance prettily at any moment on the foot that is down. If this can he, done it is a proof that the body is well poised and well carried. If the foot is put down properly the head will be carried along a perfectly level line. If the heels follow a line. then the body will advance without any side swinging of the shoulders. To put the foot down well is to walk well, to stand well, to be well.

" GOVERNOR " APPETITE

You have seen, or heard of, the apparatus in a steam- engine called the " governor." Its object is to regulate the quantity of steam supplied by the boiler to the engine, so as to keep it running evenly, whether the power required is more or less. Very good. Now, there is another governor on a machine of much greater consequence than any steam-engine—namely, the appetite, or sense of hunger, in the human body. The mill or machine that grinds the food is located in the middle of your body—down in the dark. You never can ace or touch it. You swallow your meals, and if all is right with the machine, you have no further business with it. The stomach has a peristaltic or oscillating motion,by which it shakes up and churns its contents ; but so silent and smooth is it that you have no more sense of it than of the earth's revolution. But when it is out of order, notice of the fact is served on you, even quicker than a notice to quit on non-payment of I rent. This notice • comes through the Appetite—the Governor, Manager, or Executive Officer. Yet people are foolish enough to think t at loss of appetite is bad in itself, and that they ought to do something to force it or to cola and coddle it.

Drop that notion and never pick it up again. When your appetite fails, say this : " Heigh° ! I've got a notice that my stomach does'nt want any breakfast what's thematter down there ? "

Take the experience of Mr. Walter Burkinshaw, of 230, Dunlop-street, Carbrook, Sheffield. He says that in June, 1891, his appetite fell away, and he could scarcely touch the food that was placed before him. He bad a foul taste in his mouth, and a disagreeable phlegm covered his teeth and tongue. When he did eat a mor- sel he had a deal of pain at his cheat and round the sides ; and be thought the food caused the pain, as in fact it did.

Presently he got weak, and felt tired and done up. His ears were full of singing noises,and he couldn't even hear the clock strike. When in company he says he felt miserable, because he was like a dummy ; be could'at hear what folks were saying. BF-and-by he got so weak—he is a jobbing blacksmith by trade that he had to give up work. At night he couldn't sleep munh, as he was constantly belching up wind and a sour fluid. Well, things were this way week after weak and month after month—a most dismal, unhappy, and unprofitable time, indeed.

fie took all solts of medicines, as we might expect ; " but," be says, "the physic gave me no strength." That everybody does not expect. But it is true all the same. Nothing but digested food gives any strength. The right kind of medicifte enables the stomach to digest food, and so you get strong. But let us keep to our tab% _ _ _

After telling us all the foregoing, Mr. Hurkinshaw ends his letter in these words : " When I found there was no chance of getting back to my work" (he was at Dodsworth, Barnsley, Yorkshire, when taken ill), I returned to Sheffield, and dragged on till the middle of July last (1892), when I read in a book about what Mother Seigei's Curative Syrup had done in cases like mine. I sent to Boot's Drug Stores, High-street, Atter- cliffe-road, and got the medicine. A few doses relieved me,and after I had taken three bottles I was a well man. I (Signed) Walter Burkinshaw." The date is October 15th, 1892. The address we have already named. Now, of what did Mr. Burkinshaw'a failing appetite inform him ? Simply that no mne food was wanted. Beyond that point he didn't understand what had happened. His stomach was inflamed, and—mechanic- ally speaking—dead. For a time it was like a closed f ietorc. There was a lock-out. To force down food was only to make matters worse. It was indigestion and dyspepsia, which can't be cured by ignoring it,even if yon could ignore it. But when Mother Seigel was con- sulted, and her help accepted, matters improved in a twinkling, the stomach soon resumed business, " Gov- ernor" Appetite proclaimed the fact, strength and power grew in the body, and our friend, as he says, I was a well man."

CHAPTER VII.

THE PIRATE.

While Mr. Nixon was aloft with his glass, the captain gave to his passengers a more full account of the information he had received from the cast- away. The man's story, in brief, was this:

He had been one of the crew of an American brig which had put into Port Praya for supplies. When two days' sail northwest from the Cape Verde the brig had been attacked by a pirate, robbed of everything worth taking, the crew then confined below, and the vessel fired in the cabin and in the forecastle. While the pirates had been ransacking the cabin this man had overheard a conversation between two of the freebooters, from which it appeared that their vessel had been fitted out at Santiago, and that their plan was to lie upon the course of the outward-bound Indiamen from England "And," explained Harlow, "you are probably aware that at this season of the year, with the northeast trades blowing fresh, the track of our Indiamen in this latitude is a very narrow one. When homeward-bound we strike farther to the west, and select our course from a broad meridian; but when bound this way I doubt if the last twenty ships that have made the passage have varied ten leagues in longitude at this point. So, you see, an enemy would have little difficulty in overhauling us, if so disposed. "This poor fellow heard the rascals distinctly, and he is sure that they were bound for the track of our ships. He says that the pirate vessel was a fast-sailing, American built brig, evidently fitted out originally for a slaver; that her crew were mostly Portuguese of the Cape Verde and Madeira, and over a hundred in number, and that she carried a long brass gun, probably an eighteen-pounder, amidships. "As I have already informed you, the pirates confined the plundered crew in the hold, and then set fire to the vessel and left her to her fate. The prisoners, when they knew that their brig was afire, managed to break out; but they were too late to extinguish the flames. They had time to cut the mainmast away before they were forced to leave the deck, and upon the wreck of that the crew, ten in number, with a breaker of water and bag of biscuit, took refuge. I gray God that the saved man may have op- portunity to tell us of his experience upon that ocean-tossed spar—for I have no doubt that the narrative would prove thrilling and interesting— but I can only tell you now that he alone of all his companions survives. One after another they dropped into the grave of waters, and even this last member had said his final prayers, and closed his eyes in unconsciousness, when we found him." _ _ _

"Hello 1 Aloft, there!" "Aye, a 3 e, sir 1" was responded from the fore. topmast crosstrees. "Can you make anything of that fellow yet 2" "I_think she is a brig, sir."

"Keep your eye on her." "Aye, aye, sir." "And now," said the captain, turning to his friends upon the deck, "I think we'll make pre- parations for the worst, and meantime we can hope for the best all the same."

Touching the propriety of this course there was but one opinion, and straightway all hands were mustered upon the quarter-deck. The Golden Fleece, as was the custom of the Company's ships at the time, carried four guns. They were twelve-pound carronades, and the bul- warks were so pierced that the pieces could be fixed in battery at pleasure. At present, however, two of the guns were just forward of the fore-chains —one upon each side—and the other two upon the after part of the quarter-deck ; and for these guns there was plenty of ammunition. Upon counting up those who were fit for service, Harlow found that he could muster fifty- four. This included Sir Jasper and Lord James, with six servants; Arthur Hainsford, the three lieutenants, and the surgeon, and the crew proper, which, with the petty officers, numbered forty men.

"A goodly force," -aid Sir Jasper, when he had seen the crew numbered; "and we have the advan- tage of being upon the defensive." "And we have the disadvantage," suggested the surgeon, "of having to meet men who have no mercy. " Well !" responded the old soldier, with a sig. nificant smile, if yonder follow is the pirate of which the American sailor has told us, and he proposes to fix his fangs upon us, I think we are likely to be as lightly burdened with mercy as he IL"

"Aye," said Lambert; "but our men cannot be so desperately ferocious as can those vil- lains."

"If they be true Englishmen, my dear Doctor, they can be desperately in earnest, which will answer every purpose. It is not your ferocious beast that always makes the best fight. But let us not borrow trouble. We are not yet sure—"

--- The general's speech was interrupted by a hail from aloft.

"On deck. there I" "Aye, aye! What is it ?" "She raises her topsails! She's a brig, and seems to be lying-to, with her main-topsail aback !" All on board the ship were apprehensive of the worst. _

"We must remember," offered the captain, "that any vessel, upon discovering us, would be likely to heave-to for the purpose of speaking us.,,

_ But the next report from aloft left little reason for any hope of the stranger's honesty. "On deck, there!" .. .

"Aye, aye !" "The brig has two perfect crosses in her fore- topsail. Looks as though new canvas had been patched on." "Whereabouts in the sail are they 7" "One in each clew."

The rescued man was referred to, and he recog- nised the mark at once as a peculiarity of the pirate's fore-topsail. There was litt:e more room for doubt, and without further delay Captain Harlow proceeded to arm and station his crew. He had pistols and cutlasses in plenty, besides a score or more of old muskets, which were very soon made serviceable by the application of new flints.

It will be remembered that the ship was in the north-east trades, and as her course was very near south, of course she had the wind upon the lar- board quarter. The brig, which had now raised her courses, lay directly in the Indiaman's track, with her head to the north-west and her maintop- sail to the mast. When Captain Harlow had made his arrangements below, he went aloft to where ,his lieutenant sat perched with his glass, and took an observation for himself. In a little while both he and Nixon came down and the ex- pression upon his face was answer enough for all the questions Sir Jasper had to ask. Still he told what he had seen. _

He had waited upon the crosstrees until the brig had lifted her deck to view. He had seen her weather-rail lined with men fore and alt; he had caught the gleaming of steel all in) and down the line; and once, when the brig had rolled heavily to windward, he had caught sight of a brass gun mounted amidships. "So," said Sir Jasper, "we know the work that Is before us."

"There is no way of escape ? " queried Lord Fitsjames. "The only effort we can make in that direc- tion " replied the captain, "is to haul upon the wind, and thus force the pirate to make his way dead to windward upon the chase. If we could keep out of his way until night, we might possibly avoid him under cover of the darkness. If it were later in the day there might be some hope, but we must remember that the day is nearly all before us."

—Still the ship's course was changed to the east. ward, and shortly afterwards the brig filled away and went about, standing upon the same tack.

"A chase to windward is a long chase." said the surgeon. Harlow shook his head.

"It might be so if the sailing qualities of the contestants were anywhere nearly equal. But just watch the nose of that brig, will you? See how she carries that bone in her mouth She was built for running. She lies half a point nearer to the wind than we do, and yet she goes through the water the fastest. Is it not so, Mr. Spencer ?". "It is, most assuredly," answered the first lieu- tenant, who had been thus addressed. And yet the ship kept on her way for two hours, the brig meantime tacking twice, but at the end of that time all hope of escape had been given up. Citpt, aglow had been_ soli ikloft with his glom,

LIBERAL POLITICS.

BY ME. 11. WHITHAM.

Under the above caption your gritty townsman, Mr. A. 11. Crosfield,recently put forward some state- ments to which I will, with your permission draw further attention. Ignoring the fact that I entirely disagree with his statement of the Individualistic and Socialistic positions, he would have had my heartiest sympathy had he taken up the Liberal Agfa in the good old town. Mr. Crosfield is tilting at windmills when be attacks what he is pleased to call Individualism and Socialism, and he must excuse me when I say he 'does not, in the least understand what is meant by either term. This may be his fault, or it may be the fault of those he has come in contact with in argument. Possibly it may be the fault of those he has read after. If the latter I would ask him to read the "Social horizon" if he has not already done so, and then to give •' Examiner" readers the benefit of his further research. In the meantime I will try from what I see and have seen to clear a few cobwebs away from our friend's path. We must not forget the shield has two sides, and that it very much depends upon the ground we; stand on as to what our verdict of the shield will be. I accept in all honour the interpretation our friena places on the words "Individualism " and " Socialism "as being the very truth to him. To a Socialist this is not 80, and why? simply because of Individualism we have none, and of Socialism no honest man can have any dread. Mr. Crosfield says, " Socialism and communistic vapourings, in the sense of what is subversive and destructive of individualism and intended to put an end to com- petition, we all know is impossible." If possible I will not "vapour," or deal with the question in the abstract, but rather with the subjective or shall 1 ' term it, the personal observation standpoint. In busineai, competition is the watchword, and , it is accepted that for a business man to be success- ful he must have individualism strongly developed in his character. That is, he must have strong powers of concentration, and combined with this he must have strong insistence or force of character to give the former effect. I submit that these traits are as likely to produce a Bill Sykes as a Gladstone.

Competition is the life and soul of trade. In the English character it is " Combativeness." In the French, German, or American it is simply " Ambi- tious rivalry." If the Socialists get rid of this feature in the English character, " England will be crushed politically and commercially by her ambiti- ous rivals abroad. Why was the Ship Canal built ? The railways had absorbed the already existing canals and competition was tat. Individualism concentrated in a Board of Directors became the essence of selfishness, hence the Ship Canal. State ownership of the railways would have prevented even the merest thought of a Ship Canal. A mono- poly in the manufacture of soap wou:d inevitably breed a race of selfish monopolists, as combative competion would as certainly reduce wages below the living point. State ownership of soap works would eliminate the selfish element, by manufac- turing the amount of soap required, and that of the best quality, doing away with what is a wasteful system of advertising. Competition forces the English manufacturer to keep open eyes on the continental manufacturer, and, where he can, to reduce wages to the lower level, and where he can- not to remove his plant. The jute manufacturers of Dundee and the cotton manufacturers of Lanca- shire are to day unable to compete with the pro- ducts of India where labor is only 3d. a day. I give the following extract from a speech de- livered in the Chamber of Commerce of this city : "undoubtedly Japan is our most dangerous rival, and it is but natural that China should follow in her footsteps to control with India the manufac- turing of the world. The dense population of these three countries makes the struggle for existence exceedingly keen and the rate of wages correspond- ingly low, Under the bard necessities which there prevail the science of economical living is carried to a length which even the poorest peasant of Europe cannot comprehend. As enforced frugality and the necessity for constant application are the surest enemies of shiftlessness and dissipation, the labor of the Orient is as steady, methodical and re- liable as a machine. Although in point of develop- ment the Orientals are not admitted by the white races to be their equals in intellectual alertness and physical vigor, and although unremitting ap- plication to labor and the absence of abundant nourishing food make a drain upon the vital forces, Major Pearce declares that the efficincy of Oriental skilled labor fails only 15 per cent, below that of white labor, while its wages are 90 per cent Ism" The above applies equally to the English work- man. English workmen have no idea what the Jap workman is. I see him every day, and I think I know him, and I assert that the day is not far off when the English export trade to Asia will entirely cease, and not all the combative competition the individualist of the present can bring to bear upon the question will avail. Will Mr. Crosfield tell us of the help railways give the English manufac- turers in districts where there is no competition, and in return for such information I will give him a score of cases where competition has led to cut- ting ratee,this in its turn has led to pooling profits ? We have competition, and we have no competition, as we have individualism and no individualism. ViThiteley's place in London and the "Boa March 4 " of Paris are the outgrowth of a vicious system which simply ruins the small tradesman. Wannamaker, of Philadelphia, sells books below cost, hoping to tempt custom into his mammoth store. In Indiana they pass a law forbidding this kind of individualism. In Washington they pass the "Interstate Law " prohibiting railway com- panies from conspiring to rob the public. In the Eastern States the wire manufacturers agree to pool the profits with the wire nail and barb wire manufacturer of the Weat,and your humble servant is thrown out of work. The Standard Oil Cm - pany agree with Rothschild' to "Divide the world" on the oil question,and, as a consequence, you have no competition in oil in Europe. We have none in A merica,the Standard Oil Company simply freezing out every competitor in the same way the Nettle- fold and Chamberlain Company undersold all com- petitors in the screw trade, thus asserting the superiority of individualism to competition. The ground upon which our present business methods is built is certainly too slippery for any self-respecting man to hope to stand on. And yet, these men are held up to the people as successful business men, and 'worthy of all imitation. Just recently one of our richest business men died. Two years ago he bought all the grain to be bad in this state. It has now to be sold. At the present prices this will be a loss. But his idea was to make a corner in wheat, and so further enrich himself, and had he not died he would undoubtedly have kept the people's food back until it was worthless. 8o it was with sugar. A few months ago all the sugar thieves in the country had their attention fixed on Washington. A few cents more or less in taxation made men rich , in shorter time than it takes to write it. But this thing tires one, because it is so impos- sible to point the attention to any one thing that has,even accidentally the remotest connection with lhonesty. And what is the consequence ! Who suffer from all this? Why simply we workmen. There is no single case on record where a man be- came rich by simple honest work. I grant that under the present acceptance of these terms men do become rich, and the law has no hold upon Them 'What we have now is a low cunning competition wherein the man who produces the least accumu- lates the most. Capital has accumulated in com- binations and grown to such magnitude of power as to dictate the legislation of mankind. As a re- sult of the competition of to day warehouses are groaning with their -fullness. and yet people are going without food, homes, clothes and prospects. The time is fast coming when society must destroy Ithe rich man or the rich man will destroy society. Healthy competition that would develop nature's resources for man's benefit is not to be derided, but the competition that forces fruit packers and can- ners to use dirty Chinese and Japanese help so that you in Europe may,have cheap Calfornia fruit is no way commendable. At this present time an inquiry is being prosecuted into the cause why white labour has left the fields and vineyards of this State. Go where one may, Chinese and Japenese are doing all the outside work, and of course working for what would be a mere pittance to the white man. Of course a good business man, full of that combative spirit which knows no de- feat, will get labour as he can, end as cheap, and so become the ideal fellow which some people so much admire.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 10 1895.

But let me skip across the ocean just now. I have just gone over the list of elections that have been held up to this date. What a tale comes from Derby, Manchester, Darlington, and other labour centres. It is said, and possibly Mr. Crosfield be- lieves it, that Conservative reaction has set in. do not believe it. I think that the calm before the storm is being seen. During the next few years the social forces will pull themselves together, and Liberal and Tory alike will be swept from the track. I read of ths Tory clubs in London where men are throwing chairs and tables about for sheer joy, and the walls are shaken by their delirious cheers. Speaking of London the writer says —" I should say the election will be much more corrupt than any of recent years. There used to be a certain proportion of saloon-keepers on the Liberal side,but this has come absolutely to an end. It is noted in Vie newspapers there is one large public-house in Battersea which displays John Burns' bills in colors, and this is described as probably unique in all London. When it is remembered there are over 10,000 licensed saloons in the metropolitan district this borrows great signifiance." Why is London Liberal in municipal matters and Tory when the question is one of Parliament? The answer is easy to find. Liberal principles govern the metropolis in an unselfish aid therefore econ- omical fashion. But when it comes to a question of making Imperial laws, then the rich merchant, the stockbrokers or promoters who salt the consti- tuency generously with subscriptions and dona- tions, during a contest,turn the whole liquor selling traffic into an electoral agency for their benefit. The liquor trade frightened by the point at which the " veto question had arrived in the House made a supreme effort. The Church frightened by the Welsh Disestablishment question has not dis- dained to prefer a drunken people to a free people, and has thrown in its lot with the prince of all clevils,the Liquor King. The awaking is sure to be a terrible one. But I must draw Mr.Crosfield's atten- tion to the fact, that it is the money of successful business men that has been used. Men strongly imbued with that combative and ?,ompetitive spirit Mr. Crosfield so much admires,that is preparing the land for the baptism of fire that is sure to come. 27i 13th San Francisco.

DROLL BLUNDERS.

As specimens of typographical errors, there are, amongst others, the following :—By the insertion of one letter in place of another, a newspaper, not long since, reporting the danger that an express train had run, In consequence of a cow getting upon the line, said : " As the safest way, the engineer put on full steam, dashed up against the cow, and literally cut it into calves ! "A Scotch newspaper, reporting the speeches at a Scott centenary meeting,mamle one of the orators exclaim with more truth than accuracy : "0, Caledonia, stern and wild, Wet nurse for a poetic child."

Never, perhaps, was the word " austere" more mis- construed than in the instance of a clergyman in Lan- cashire, who got a wholesome warning in regard to pul- pit articulation by discovering in one house which he visited the day after preaching from St. Luke xix. 21. that the servant had gone home with the impression that his text had been : " I feared thee, because thou art an oyster-man! " A Hampshire incumbent recently reported in the " Pall Mall Gazette "some of the blunders he had heard made in the marriage service by that class of persons who have to pick up the words as best they can, from hearing them repeated by others. He said that in his own parish it was quite the fashion for the man, when giving the ring, to say to the woman: " With my body I thee wash up, and with all my hurdle goods I thee and thou." He said the women were generally better up in this part of the service than the men. One day, however, a bride startled him by promising, in what she supposed to be the language of the Prayer Bopk, to take her husband " Te-'ave and to 'old from this day fortni't for betterer horse, for risherer power, in siggerness health, to love cherries, and toloay." What meaning this extraordinary vow conveyed to her own mind the incumbent said it baffled him to conjecture. . . . . .

A ludicrous story is told of a bAilie, whose studies in natural history seem to kave been rather limited. The following case came before him one day :—" A man who kept a ferret, having to go into the country,left the cage with the ferret in charge of a neighbour till he should return. The neighbour incautiously •opened the cage door, and the ferret escaped. The owner was very angry and brought a claim against his neighbour for damages. The following was the decision of the learned bailie : ' Nae doot,' he said to the man who had been left in charge, ye was wrong to open the cage door ' ; but, he added, turning to the other, 'Ye was wrong tae, for why did ye no clip the brute's wings?' " It is also told of a certain Glasgow bailie that, whec visiting Paris as one of a deputation from Glasgow to Louis Philippe, the king said, when showing the party through his library, where he had many of the English classics : " You will know Milton very well ?" ' 0, bless you, yes; bless you, yes," said the bailie, che r- fully, delighted that something had been mentioned that he did know. " Yes, your majesty, I know Milton very well" (Milton is a little place in the neighbourhood of Glasgow) ; " we're just building slaughter-houses there !"

By the bad arrangement of clauses in composition, ludicrous blunders are sometimes made. A Wisconsin paper announced that the Board of Education had " re- arOved to erect a building large enough to accommodate 500 students three stories high" In an English paper an advertisement appeared, under the heading of" To Let." of " A house for a family in good repair." " Punch " noted this, and conjectured that " a family in good repair " must mean one in which none of the members were cracked " The brooches would have been sent before but have been unwell," was a note of apelogy sent to Dean Alford by his jeweller ; and "Two sisters want washing" was an advertisement which appeared in the " Manchester Guardian."

An amusing style of blunder is the "bull," for which the Irish get most credit. It was an Irish editor that exclaimed, when speaking of the wrongs of his country, " Her cup of misery has been cyerilowing, and is not yet full !" It was an Irish newspaper that said of Robes- pierre that " he left no children behind him, except a brother, who was killed at the same time." Irish also was the cornet who, when writing home from India praising the much abused climate as really one of the best under the sun, added:—" But a lot of young fellows come cut here, and they drink and they eat, and they eat and they drink, and they die ; and then they write home to their friends, saying it was the clinaate that did it I"

Though not so numerous as those of the Emerald Isle, Scotland is not without its specimens of this kind of blunder. Two operatives in one of the Border towns were heard disputing about a new cemetery, beside the elegant railing of which they were standing. One of them, evidently disliking the Continental fashion in which it WAS being laid out, said in disgust :—"l'd rather dee than be buried in sic a place." "Wee!, it's the vera reverse wr me," said the other ; "for I'll be buried naewhere else, if I'm spared." A story of Dean Ramsay's is given of a half-cracked man in the parish kirk of "Auld Ayr," who got his head in between the iron rails in front of a seat, and Startled the congregation by crying out in the middle of the sermon :—" Murder, murder ! my head'll hoe to be cuttit aff. Holy minister ! 0, my head mann be cuttit off. It's a judgment for leaving my godly Mr. Peebles (his former minister) at the Newton." When he had been extricated and quieted, and was asked why he put his head there, he said : " It was find to look on wi' another woman.'" - -

A doctor who had one day allowed himself to drink too much wa9 sent for to see a fashionable lady who was ailing. He sat down by the bedside,took out his watch, and began to count her pulse as well as his obfuscated condition would permit. He counted : "One, two, three; "then he got confused, and began again : "One, two, three, four." Still confused, he began again : "One, two." No ; he could not do it. Thoroughly ashamed of himself, he shut up his watch, muttering: " Tipsy, I declare—tipsy! " Staggering to his feet he told the lady to keep her bed and take some hot lemon- ade, to throw`her in a perspiration, and he would see her next day. In the morning hp received tbei following note from the lady, marked " Private " :—" Dear Doc- tor,—You were right. I dare not deny it. But lam thoroughly ashamed of myself, and will be more careful for the future. Please accept the enclosed fee fcr your visit" (a ten pound note)," and do not I entreat of you. breathe a word about the state in which you found me," The lady, in fact, had herself been drinking too much, and catching the doctor's murmured words,thought they referred to her. He was too far gone to see what was the matter with his patient, and she too far to observe that the doctor was in the same condition !

- There are some gird stories about mistakes as to ner- son.lt is said that William IV.was once kept waiting out- side a certain part of Windsor Castle,owing to a private entrancebeing that evening in charge of a substitute who did not know the king in his plain clothes. .4 you can't pass, old 'un," said the man, cheerfully. " No one is allowed to pass here after dark, except the king and the lamplighter." A mutual mistake was once made by Lord Guildford and a lady of quality in the house of Lord Melville. There was a dinner parry at which Lord Seaforth Waft to be present. As Seaforth was deaf and dumb, Lady Mel- ville, before the company arrived,sent a lady friend whn was familiar with the dumb alphabet into the drawing room, to be ready against his lordship's arrival. It happenee, however, that Lord Guildford was The first to make his appearance; and the lady, taking him for Lord Seaforth, began to sign to him nimbly with her fin era. His lordship, happening to be an adept in the deaf and dumb alphabet, replied in the same way ; and so they went on talking in this noiseless manner on their fingers till Lady Melville entered, when her friend said aloud : " Well. I have been talking rn7 best to this dumb man!" " Dumb red .d Lord Guildford in unfeigned surprise ; " why, my good lady, I thought you were dumb !"

WARRINGTON RUGBY FOOTBALL SPORTS.

The eleventh annual athletic festival and gala of the Warrington Vootball Club were held on Monday at the football field, Wilderspoul Causeway. The day although cloudy and threatening, fortunately kept off rain, and as a consequence there was a very large "gate," the number present being about 6,000. There was a splendid programme provided and the whole of the arrangements were carried through without a hitch. The officials were Mr. T. Bowers, clerk of the course ; Mr. E. Kane, starter; Messrs. J. Evans, W. Lomas, R. Andrews and E. Gilbert, judges ; Sergeant Major Haigh, judge of the boxing competition ; Messrs. T. Davies, W. Hewitt, W. Hill, W. Hayes, F. Barber, C. Burton, I. Racket, J. T. Bate, J. Lawless and H. Ashton, commit- tee ; Mr. W. Platt, handicapper; Mr. T. Pemberton, hon. treasurer; Mr. J. E. Warren, hon. secretary; Mr. S. W. Jeffery, assistant hon. secretary. NORTHERN COUNTIES GYMNASTIC CHAMPIONSHIP. —The N.C.A.e . again favoured Warrington, as they did last year, with the competition for the gymnastic champion- ship of the Northern Counties. There were two prizes, viz., a gold and silver medal. The competition was worked by two styles (one strength and one swing) on each apparatus, and in case the judge required it, the three or more competitors who had gained the high- est scores should compete for "set " tricks. The judge on Monday was Mr. J. Wall, et Manchester. There had been six entries, but as only two, E. Halton, Manchester Y.M.C.A., and J. Vernon, Warrington Gymnasium, put in an appearance, the "set" tricks were not required. The result of the competition was that Halton gained the first prize, and Vernon the second, the points gained being Halton,horizontal bar,strength 9, swinging 7, parallel bar, strength 7, swinging 84, total points, 314. Vernon, horizontal bar, strength 64, swinging 3, parallel bar. strength 6, swinging 4, total 194. 100 YARDS HANDICAP.—For this event there were three prizes and 38 entries, of which 30 took part. LEAGUE OF THE ORCSS. EXCURSION TO LIVERPOOL AND NEW BRIGHTON.

On Sunday (writes a correspondent) the War- rington League of the Cross went with numerous friends for a sail to New Brighton via Ship Canal. The League band accompanied the excursion. The rendezvous was the League Hall, Bewsey-et., from whence the party proceeded to Latchford locks at 11 a.m., playing in fine style a selection from Mozart's twelfth Mass. All along the route was crowded with excursionists full of spirits(not Jame- son's) The "Eagle" arrived at Latchford at about a quarter to twelve from Manchester, and it, was a busy quarter of an hour loading her with her human freight. The steamer was crowded from stem to stern, every available inch being occupied. There must have been about two thousand people en board. The Ship Canal people would do a wise thing, if they placed chains along the lock, especi- ally if they intend catering for these excursions; else some day there will be a calamity. During the voyage which occupied about eight hours, there and back, the League band under the conductorship of Mr. John Mullins,president of the League,discoursecl sweet music, which was a source of great enjoyment to all on board the Eagle. The scenery along the route is very fine, especially on the Cheshire side' the weather was all that could be desired, and all seemed to enjoy themselves thoroughly. The stay at New Brighton was necessarily a short one, but no doubt the " Ham and Egg" parade received ample proof of the digestive powers of the Warrington- ians. The return journey was very pleasant indeed. The wind having gone down consicterablv, all was calm and delightful ; but when the steamer arrived at the new cutting, tha band struck up in a soft pianissimo, Cardinal Newman's beautiful hymn, "Lead kindly light, Amid th' encircling gloom Lead Thou me on." The people sang these beautiful words softly, almost in a whisper, the moon shining placidly upon the waters, the while our steamer glided I softly into the harbour. Then the whole concourse of people joined in Father Faber's beautiful hymn, " Faith of our Fathers living still." Thus ended a day long to be remembered by all who had the good fortune to be there. Great praise is due to the committee for the admirable arrange- ments due to their energy and foresight ; but especial praise is due to Mr James Whittaker. band secretary who is the very life and soul of all good works in connection with the League of the Cross.

HOUSEHOLD LINENS, hemmed and marked free of charge.— J. and W. DUTTON, Sankey-SI., Warringtea.—Advt. FLOWER BERVICIL AT FADOEITE.

The annual flower service in connection with Christ Church was held on Sunday, there being a good congregation. The Sunday school children attended, each child carrying a bunch of beautiful flowers. During the singing of a hymn the children walked in single file to the altar rails, and handed their flowers to the Rev. J. J. Langham and Mr. Joseph Slather, who placed them around the altar. Offerings of fruit were also made. An adiress was delivered by the Vicar from the words " The trees of the Lord are full of sap." The flowers and fruit have since been sent to the Warrington Infirmary for use in the sick wards.

The annual excursion of the Padgate Wesleyan Church choir took place on Saturday afternoon, the place selected being Sandiway Head on the Dela- , mere Forest. Although much rain fell in Warring- ton and district it was not the case at the forest, and needless to say the members of the choir thoroughly enjoyed themselves. The journey was made by 'bus, Sandiway Head being reached about 2,30 p.m. After rambling about for some time the trippers adjoiarned to the Blue Cap Inn, where tea was provided. The return journey was commenced about eight o'clock, and Padgate reached at 10 30 p.m. The trustees of the chapel provided the nec- essary funds for the trip. PIDGATE CHURCH SUNDAY SCHOOL TRIP. The annual trip ot the Padgate Church Sunday scholars took place on Friday. They were con- veyed, together with a number of their parents, in five large 'buses to Frodsham. A start was made about twelve o'clock noon. Frodsham being reached about 2 p.m. The children rambled upon the hills and otherwise enjoyed themselves until four o'clock where tea was partaken of at Mersey View. This over, the children again engaged in games until 6 p.m., when a start was made for home, Padgate being reached about nine o'clock, everyone being thoroughly satisfied with the outing. The trip was accompanied by the Rev. J. J. Langham (vicar) Rev. W. R.Hartwright (cnrate-in-charge of Orford), Mrs. Beevers, Mr. Frank Blackwell, and the most of the Sunday school teachers. THE WESLEYAN CHRISTIAN ENDEAVOUR SOCIETY.

The second picnic of the Christian Endeavour held their second outing on Monday. The !place chosen this year was Oulton Park passing through Delamere Forest on the way. Assembling between 8.30 and 9 a.m. the tops of the 'buses were soon crowded, some unfortunates having to travel inside, but if an exuberance of boisterous mirth is a true test of pleasure, then theinside passengers were the happiest company. The destination was reached by 12 a.m., and after lunch the party separated, one portion making the tour ot the gardens and the other exploring the park, first proceeding to the gardens. Through the kindness of Mr. Parkinson several photos ot the two groups were taken. After tea had been disposed of, a number of the visitors inspected the church, ascending into the belfry, whereupon a few tried their 'prentice bands upon the bell ropes, and the more adventurous ascended to the top of the tower, from which a splendid view was obtained of the surrounding country. The return journey was commenced at 7.30 p.m., and the las; twelve miles were covered in a downpour of rain, which however, did not damp the ardour of the young travellers. THE CHURCH LIDS' BRIGADE IN CAMP.

The 2nd ( Warrington) Battalion Liverpool Regiment of the Church Lads' Brigade joined the northern camp at South Shore, Blackpool, on Satur- day. A special train left Bank Quay Station at 2.30 p.m., containing the Warrington Battalion and the Sale and Altrincham Companies, and at Wigan they were joined by the Wigan (Ist Liverpool) Battalion, together with the camp headquarter'a staff. The Warrington members numbered 125 in all, and were made up as follows :—Lieutenant- Colonel T. D. Buckton, commanding, and Captain F. M. Appleton. adjutant. No. 1 Company (St. James', Latchford), Lieutenant C. Slater and five lads; No. 2 Company (Padgate), six lads (Captain Appleton); No. 3 Company (St. Elphin's), Captain Willis, Lieutenant Willis, and 16 lade; No. 4 Com- pany (Great Sankey), Lieutenants Ringer and Owen, and 52 lads; and No. 5 Company (St. Peter's), Lieutenant Brookes, and 19 lads. Captain Mcliechnie, of Great Sankey met the battalion at Blackpool. The battalion brass band from Great Sankey, consisting of 18 members, also accompanied the party. South Shore was reached without mis- hap about five o'clock, and the lads were not long before they were settled down and did justice to the tea provided by the excellent Quartermaster, Captain Crawley. The lads are well provided for, and every precaution is taken to avoid accidents, and all are looking forward to a very happy week under canvas. HOUSEHOLD Lianas, hemmed and marked free of charge.— J. and W. Dutton, Sankey-s.. Virarrington.—Adnt. SHOCKING ACCIDENT IN BRIDGE-STREET.

At about five o'clock on Tuesday evening a very serious accident occurred in Bridge-street. It appears that when opposite the tobacco stores of Mr. Mann a collision occurred between a heavily- laden lurry, owned by Messrs.Greenall, Whitley and Co.. and a light milk float, belonging to Mr. Wibberley, milk dealer. The effect of the accideut was that the milk float —which was being driven by Mrs. Wibberley—was twisted completely round ,the head of the pony, as well as the top of both shafts, crashing through the plate-glass window of Mr. Mann's shop. The occurrence occasioned great excitement in the thoroughfare, and traffic was for many minutes impeded. A few minutes after the crash had happened, a son of Mr. Shaw, a neigh- bouring butcher, appeared on the scene, and seeing that the poor animal's head was lodged in such a painful position, at once began to further break the glass in order to free it. This he succeeded in doing, but meanwhile the animal had been severely cut, and bled profusely from its wounds. It was immediately taken to Mr. Ferguson, veterinary surgeon, of Rylands-street, where it was profession- ally treated. The incident so much affected Mrs. Wibberley that she was seized with a faint, and attention had also to be accordingly directed to her. A great deal of the stock which was in Mr. Mann's 1 window at the time was spoiled, and the damage to this, and also to the plate-glass window is estimated 1 at more than £l2. _

AMERICLNIROCKER CHAIRS, sale price, 12s. 6d. B LIMO TANLIne. with two trays. sale price, 4s. Ild.—J. and W. DuTTox, Sankey-it., Warrington.— Advt. CORONATION OF THE DAHLIA QUEEN IT B URTONWOOD.

The coronation of ilie-Dahliti-Oneen at Burton- wood took place on Monday in a field adjoining the Bridge Inn, and it being the first occasion on which such a sight had been witnessed in the village large numbers of people paid a visit to the field, not only the residents of Burtonweod, but persons from Warrington, Earlestown, 1.0, Newton, and other places. The weather was charming, and the pro- ceedings passed off in a very pleasant manner. The first item on the programme was a procession through the village and round Collins Green. This consisted of the scholars from the various schools in the district, and different trades were also repre- sented. One of the most striking features in the procession was a lurry on which colliers were de- picted in the act of getting coal. During the parade large quantities of bunting were to be seen across the streets, flags being displayed from the windows of the houses. After walking through the village and round Collins' Green the processionists adjourned to the field, where at five o'clock, the Queen, Miss Horrohin, Burtonwood, was crowned by Miss Jackson, of Bank House. Burtonwood, who also presented her with a beautiful Bible. Morris and other dances were given by several children, and at the conclusion of the crowning ceremony,tea was served in the field, and the remainder of the day was spent in dancing and games of various kinds. The Penketh brass band was in attendance, and played a selection of popular airs. The whole of the arrangements, which were in the hands of Mr. George Anders, proprietor of the Bridge Inn, ' Burtonwold,were carried.out in a very satisfactory manner, and the event was a great success, and will no doubt be looked for another year with much interest.

STRANGE DEATH ON A BOY.

The death has been reported to the police of Patrick Varley, four years of age, who resided with hia parents at 90, Pierpoint-street, off Be wsey-road. It appears that the lad sustained an injury to his elbow a few days ago, and was removed to the Infirmary, where he remained until he died on Saturday morning. No inquest will be held, as the doctor who attended him certified that the death was due to natural causes and not to the accident. _INJURY TO A WINDOW CLEANER,

Mary Eastens, living at—FairCloUgh's-yard, Bridge Foot, was admitted to the Infirmary on Saturday afternoon, suffering from a broken leg. It appears that she was standing on a stool, which she had placed on a chair, when it slipped from under her and she fell to the ground. Her screaming attracted the attention of a few neighbours, who immedi- ately went to her assistance. 'I hey picked her up, when it was found that one of her legs wns broken. The injured woman was speedily removed to the Infirmary:

On Sunday. a large number of persons spent the afternoon in a pleasant four hours' cruise on the Ship Canal, from L3tchford Locks to Runcorn, Wes- ton Point, and Saltport, The canal company's saloon paddle steamer, the "Flying Falcon," being cbartered for the occasion. SUDDEN DEATH.

The death took place on Sunday morning at his home, 8. Jolley's-yard, Friars'-green, of Thomas Welsh. 58 years of age. The deceased, who was a tailor by trade, and oecasionally went to public- houses flute-playing, had been suffering for about three months from bronchitis and had been attended by a doctor. On Saturday night he went to two or three houses with his instrument, and OR returning home,about 10.30, he complained of pains on the chest. He took some medicine and went to bed about 11 o'clock. On his wife awaking next morning she found him dead. No inquest will be held. ATTEMPTED SUICIDE AT WARRINGTON. James Evans, a single man, 43 years of age, for many years past employed at Longford gasworks, attempted to commit suicide by cutting his throat with a razor at his residence, 78, Longford-street, Warrington, between 12 and 1 o'clock on Saturday afternoon. Evans had been drinking lately, and on his cousin (with whom he resides) taking his dinner tolhim, he found him in a weak condition and bleeding profusely. Dr. Nuttall was called in and dressed the wound, and Evans was conveyed in a cab to the infirmary. The wound was about four inches long.

ACCIDENT TO A POSTMAN AT BANK QUAY STATION.

On Friday light week, Thomas Austin, aged fifty-two, residing at 112, School Brow,Warrington, whilst following his employment as a mailmaa,met with a serious accident at the London and North- Western Railway Station, Bank Quay. Austin was engaged pulling a hand truck heavily laden with mailbags, when owing to the defective condition of the paving of the platform, the truck came sud- denly to a standstill, and the rebound knocked Austin down on the edge of the platform. The 11.30 mail train from Euston was entering the station, and the footboards caught him by the waist and dragged tim a few yards. Austin lay jammed between the platform and the footboards. He was only with great difficulty released from his perilous position. The injured man was taken to the

A FALSE REPORT OF THE DROWNING OP THREE CHILDREN. During the course of Tuesday afternoon a report spread about the town that three children had got in the river somewhere near the iron bridge and been drowned. The children's names were stated to be Crank, Ogden and Basnett,all three belonging to Dutton-at., off Mersey-st. Happily the report proved to be totally unfounded. It appears that the children were missing from home,and could not be found anywhere in the neighbourhood The mother of one of them enquired from a playmate as to their whereabouts, and he is stated to to have said that he had seen them fall into the river. No clear story, however,could be got from him. All the lad seemed able to say was that the children had fallen into the river near the iron bridge. An alarm was given, and the police made enquiries at `A, ar- rington bridge, but no one bad seen the children or seen anyone in the river. Meanwhile the parents of the children became most anxious, and as time went on they really began to think that some mis- hap had befallen their offspring. A crowd assembled in Dutton-st.' and the mothers were greatly sympa- thised with by the neighbours and friends in their supposed loss. No tidings could be gleaned of the missing children,but as the police failed to find any trace of their having got into the river, it was hoped that they would ultimately turn up. This they did late in the afternoon, to the great joy of their parents. It seems the children who are from three to five years of age, had been lost, and after wandering about for a time they were put on the way home, and arrived there utterly unconcious of the anxiety they had caused their parents.
