WARRINGTON CYCLING CLUB.

PRESIDENT'S DAY.

On Saturday last the members of the Warrington Cycling Club mustered at their head-quarters at the Norton Arms Hotel for an afternoon in the coun- try on what is known amongst the initatied as " President Day." Under lair conditions as to weather, cycles are of course indispensable for the run,which this year,as on several previous occasions, was along the picturesque Cheshire highroads to the village of Cranage beyond Knutsford. The Presi- dent and guests, not necessarily being cyclists, are of course, accommodated with a suitable convey- ance. But on Saturday the elements were so im- propitious that at the last moment cycles were per- force abandoned and recourse was had to convey- ances for the whole party. A splendid four-horse wagonette was bespoken for the rank and file of the members—the president and visitors following in a two-horse landau all from the well equipped satbles of Messrs Maginn Bros. There was, however, very little rain after leaving the town and the roads, three miles out, were found to be in excellent order despite the heavy showers that that had been prevalent in the district. A pleasant outward run was mifde with stoppages at Hoo Green and Knutsford and the destination was reached soon after six o'clock. The swan Farm Cranage is well-known to both home End distant cyclists as affording all reasonable com- forts and the genial character of the host and hostess, Mr. and Mrs. Rossan, are not less widely appreciated, and when the attractions of a rosebud garden of charming daughters are thrown into the scale it is not difficult to account for the popu- larity of this halting place with the more impres- sionable and susceptible wheelmen. A varied and substantial meal here awaited the party and being splendidly served was done ample justice too , Mr. Morris, the ever-popular president, made on behalf of the Club, and in his characteristic kind and hearty manner, a presentation of a silver thimble to each of the ladies of the house. After these and other formal proceedings a few songs were indulged in by the Misses Rosson and Mr. Whittle, the well-known Warrington humouroua vocalist who was one of the party. Some photo- graphic groups were then taken on the lawn in front of the house, by the enterprising and now- almost übquitous "amateur photographer," and soon after seven the party set forth on the home- ward journey well pleased with their short stay at the homestead of the Rosson's, and with many and prolonged leave-takings. At Knutsford a consider- able stop was made to bait and rest the horses and the time was pleasantly occupied at the Red Cow where there is ample accommodation for man and beast. The party took possession of the assembly room in connection with the ' establishment and diverted themselves with music and the drinking of appropriate toasts. In these proceedings the geniality of the President and the officials of the club and the good feeling existing between one and all, were conspicuously apparent. Mr. Morris, Mr. Pickavance, Mr. Sampson, Mr. Birtles, Mr. Waddington and Mr. Jeffrey each con- tributed expressions of goodwill and of their en- joyment of the outing and spoke of the good results of cycling as a health giving exercise and a refin- ing pastime. These proceedings were interspersed with several songs from our local chevalier, Mr. Whittle, who was in good form and was the means of creating much of that hilarity which marked the late hours of the outing. Mr Jeffrey also played some sparkling pianoforte items which were much enjoyed. "Auld Lang Syne ' having been sung by the whole company with joined hands, the party again took the road to Warrington which was reached shortly before midnight.

A DOCTOR'S DOUBTS CuNQUERED.

The "News of the World" publishes an interesting piece of "Special Commission" work by a medical ex- pert. He began, with a frankly avowed scepticism, by interviewing Miss E. Taylor, a charming young lady, of 2, Parkmottages, Hendon-lane, Fmchley, London. The illness, which (as the medical commissioner explains) meant, if not stopped, nothing less than death by con- sumption or decline, was of tour years' standing. It • was a severe case of chronic ansemia, and it had been treated medically in vain. The information obtained Speedily showed—first, that it was a clear case of anaemia; and, secondly, that a wonderful cure had been effected. The girl had outgrown her strength. Loss Of strength, flushing of the face, nervousness, and more or less hysterical symptlins were coupled with all the attendant complications, medical details of which were given and verified. Loss of appetite came as a natural sequence, and the patient suffered all the inevitable consequences of armemia. This was the position of affairs when the patient's mother learned what had been effected in some similar cases by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. She gave them a trial. After taking six boxes she gave up the remedy, and now far over six weeks her mother and sister assure me there has not been the slightest sign of the trouble. The general health has improved, the old distressed feel- ing has gone. She has never enjoyed such good health in her life.

STOCKTOIsi HEAT.

APPLETON PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE.—A meeting of this Committee was held on Friday, last week, under the pre- sidency of Mr. H. Eyes. There were also present,Messrs. J. Whitlow, Coppock, Hampson, Britch, and Ashton Hayes, Mr. Farrington (inspector of nuisances), Mr. I Swinton (surveyor), and Mr. Ashton (clerk). A letter I was read from Mr. Marshall Stevens of the Manch'ester' Ship Canal Company, stating that he had made inquiry- into the statement that the Stockton Heath Bridge was open on the morning of ths 26:h ult. from 7-5 till 7.25. a period of 20 minutes, and found that the Rural Council had been misinformed in the matter, as the bridge was only open from 7-15 to 7 25 or a period of 10 minutes,as would be seen from a copy of the log enclosed showing the times the bridge was open on the date named. He thought under the circumstances the resolution passed by the Council should be rescinded and that in fairness to the Canal Company, full inquiry should be made into any future complaints that were made before such reso- lutions were adopted. It was considered by the Commit., tee that the complaint they had received was from a satisfactory source,and allowed the resolution to remain as originally adopted.—Mr Potts, clerk of the Cheshire County Council, in a communication,stated that he was instructed by the Rivers' Pollution Committee of the Council to call their attention to the pollution by them of the disused pretion of the river Mersey in the neighbourhood of Wilderspool and Walton,. and to intimate that unless steps were taken by the Runcorn Rural Council to prevent the nuisance which arose from such pollution, they would have to consider- ' the advisability of obtaining the necessary powers to 'enable them to put in operation the Rivers' Pollution Prevention Act in the portion of the river Mersey referred to. tie would be obliged by the Clerk bringing the matter under the notice of the Rural Council and by intimating to him the steps they purposed taking to. remedy the pollJtion complained of.—lt was resolved that a copy of the correspondence be forwarded to the Corporation of Warringten and that the clerk of the County Council be informed that that step had been taken.—Writing on the 27th July, with reference to Appleton water supply, Mr. Robert Davies said he could confirm much of what Mr. Hall had said reaarding it, but his house was a little above his (Mr. Davies') in altitude, and he was much worse off. The scheme the late Mr. Marson, an old waterworks directoroventioned, was to have a tower erected on high around south of the Bridgewater Canal from which a sufficient water supply could be afforded to those who were in want of it. This ought not to cost much, and it would serve a wide area. Tha water at Mr. ?damn's old house , seemed to have been beyond the influence of the %lan chrster Ship Canal. and was, he believed, undisturbed by it, and there was water at :-Jr. Lvon's quarry, which would, he suppo9ed be available if Winwick could not supply them. A letter was also read on the same sub- ject from Mr. John Ellison, clerk of the Parish Council of Appleton. in which he stated that at a meeting of the Appleton Parish Council held on the 23rd ult., the fol- lowing resolution was unanimously agreed to :—" That in view of the assurance of the Warrington Corporation to provide an efficient supply of water to Birchdale (in the parish of Appleton) in la near future, the Parish Council considered that no action need be taken at pre- sent."—lt was resolved that this latter recommendation be forwarded to Mr. Robert Davies —rhe minutes were confirmed at the meeting of the Runcorn Rural Council on Tuesday.

SHOCKING TRAGEDY AT A MANCHESTER STATION.

London Road Station. Manchester, was the scene of a painful tragedy on Monday night. A man named' Brooks, a native of Worcestershire, came to Manches- ter on Monday to see his wife end daughter, from whom he had lived apart for some time. He induced the two to accompany him to the railway station about nine o'clock at night, when he said he should return to Worcestershire. On pretence of Poking for his railway ticket, which he was supposed to have dropped into the- lavatory. he induced his daughter to accompany him there. He then suddenly drew a butcher's knife which he had rwncealed and cut the girl's throat so severely that she died half an hour afterwards. Brooks is in custody.

RUNCORN AN]) WIDNES.

FORTHCOMING CHOIR SERMONS. The Primitive Methodist Chapel choir sermons will be preiched on Sunday morning and evening by Mr. J. Peters, of Buckley. The choir will render special music, and collections on behalf of the choir fund will be taken at each service.

TONIC SOLFA COLLEGB.-LOCAL SUCCESSES. -

At the quarterly examinations held in connection with the:above in June last, Miss Hannah Moores a Runcorn, and Mr. Edward Owen Evans of Widnes. weri success- ful in passing for the matriculation certificate. FOUR CHILDREN AT A BIRTH.

The wife ot Mr. James Clarke, who is employed at the Salt Union Works, Weston Point, and resides at the riolloway, Runcorn, gave birth to two sons and two diughters on Saturday All the children were stillborn. The mother is plogressing favour- ably. MISSION SERVICES AT RUNCORN.

Open air ;WC; in connection with Camden Mission Band, Runcorn, were held on Sunday. Mrs. Harrison and Miss Nellie Wright conducted A service held is Prince-st.,while at that in Cooper-st. Messrs. John Dean and T. Heath, ably assisted by several friends,officiated. There were good congregations, and the addresses given were of an interesting description. Solos were occa- sionally sung. RUNCORN PETTY SESSIONS. . .

Owing to Bata—Yo—lidaY.,—Cli-es—e—sessio-Os were not held until Tuesday, when the magistrates who occupied the bench, were :—Mr. T. Handley (chairman) and Mr. E. Imison.—The license of the Prospect Inn, Weston, was transferred from William Owen to John Vase, and a transference of the Weaver Hotel license was made for John Vase to Oliver Dunbavin.—William Horatio Fletcher, a painter, of Runcorn was committed to gaol for one month on account of arrears, amounting to over £3, on a mainteaance order made by the magistrates in April last. STATIONS OF LOCAL WESLEYAN__MINISTESS,

The following local stations of Wesleyan ministers have been confirmed :—Runcorn, Thomas Brackenbury (superintendent), William George Walton), C. de P. Gliddon (Frodsham), Robert Renton, who shall change on two Sundays in every quarter with the ministers of the Runcorn circuit, Frederick F. Edmunds, supernu- merary; Widnes, John W. Silcox, Charles J. Preston ; Sc. Helens and Prescot, William Harrowsmith, John Bartley. The letter of resignation through ill-health from the Rev.'. Lile Stone who was staticned at Halton, was received with regret by the Conference, and a letter of sympathy was ordered to be sent to him.

===== 0002601_18950810_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
CADETS OF TEMPERANCE PICNIC..

The annual picnic in connection with the Runcorn Cadets of Temperance was held on Saturday, when a large number proceeded to Overton Hill. The following ladies and gentlemen accompanied the trippors, and did everything to contribute to the happiness and success of the treat :—Mesdames Cook and Bisbrown, Messrs. Edward Key, Thomas Whyte, Thomas Cooke, John Jones, George D. Holt, and Daniel Bisbrewn. An excel- lent tea was provided by Mrs. Parker,at Mersey View,to which full justice was' done. After tea a variety of games was indulged in, and nuts and sweets were given to the children. The weather was fine, and the young- sters enjoyed themselves very much,the party returning home soon after nine o'clock.—At a meeting of the Cadet Committee, under the presidency of Mr. Thomas Cooke, it was moved by Mr. Edward Key, and seconded by Mr. Thomas Whyte, that the best thanks of the society be given to those gentlemen who had so generously given the treat. This was supported by Mr. Bisbrown in terms of warm appreciation and unanimously passed.

BANK HOLIDAY AT RUNCORN.

There was a general exodus from Runcorn on Bank Holiday. There were no attractions whatever in the town, and conEe7quently the inhabitants proceeded in large numbers by rail and water to pastures new. An excursion along the Bridgewater Canal to London Bridge and Grappenball under the auspices of the Runcorn branch of the Mersey Mission to Seamen, was exceed- ingly well patronised. Two boat loads of excursionists left Top Locks, Runcorn, about nine a.m. During the journey light refreshments were provided, and at Grap- penhall an excellent tea was partaken of in the schoolroom. The excursirmists took advantage of the permission of Mr. R. A. Naylor to inspect the grounds connected with his residence, which is a short distance from the Latchford Locks. Runcorn was reached in the evening about nine p.m., a very enjoyable day having been spent. A word of praise is due to Mr. William Shaw (missionary) for his excellent arrangements. About 150 persons departed from Run- corn by rail, to North Wales, 350 to Liverpool, 130 to Chester, and over 500 to Frodsham. The weather at Runcorn was fine until about six p.m. when rain fell heavily, and continued until about mid-night. THE NEW VOTERS' LISTS FOR RUNCORN.

It will be well for every Liberal in Runcorn to ex- amine the new list of electors for Runcorn North and South, and to see if his name is placed thereon and pro- perly described. The lists will be found affixed to the church and chapel doors and other public places. It may be stated that the register for 1895, up to the present, contains the names of 3,179 persons, as against 3,212 in 1894.. The numbers in the respective wards are as follows :—Halton ward, 1894, 384 ; 1895, 371 •, Mersey ward, 531, 524 ; Central ward, 772, 732 ; Bridgewater ward. 583, 576 ; Newtown ward,s6B, 556 ; Heath ward, 374, 421. It will be noticed that with the exception of the latter ward there is a decrease in the number of electors as compared with 1894.

THINGS HEARD- AND SEEN AT RUNCORN.

BY OUR RUNCORN CORRESPONDENT,

This week some plain words must be said about the co-operators of Runcorn and district, who on Tuesday evening seemed to be doing anything but co-operating for the welfare of the society of which they are members. There seemed to be a total lack of co-operation and a disposition to divide into two camps, those on the Cheshire side of the river form- ing one, and those from Widnes, another. If this practice is continued it will end in a split. The Runcorn Society is parent of the numerous branches which have been established in the dis- trict, and which now number 14. Its career has been a most prosperous one and a credit to al: con- cerned, but if any feeling exists amongst the With flea members that they couid manage the affairs better themselves, it would appear to be a wiser course to see if some amicable arrangement could be made rather than to allow this antipathy to con- tinue.

At a meeting of the members on Tuesday, there was quite an unnecessary amount of squabbling about the revision of the rules. As a matter of fact the revised rules which have been laboriously pre• pared by the authorised committee, have not yet been submitted to the members for their inspection, and it was apparently idle and mischievous to discuss and forecast them. A matter which added not a little piquancy to the situation was the information that a voluntary committee at Run- corn had been revising rules for themselves, and what was more, this voluntary committee was elected to supersede the committee which had been authorised to do the work. What ,a "shine '' there will be on the 20th, when a special meeting for the revision of rules is held !

One of the most remarkable features of this very remarkable meeting was the resolution which was carried to increase the dividend from 2s. 9d. to 34. in the The former sum was recom- mended by the committee, and would have allowed of a small sum being added to the reserve fund. But this recommendation was thrown to the winds ' and it was decided to increase the dividend as stated at the expense of the reserve fund. Over £5OO will thus be taken from this fund, and the least that can be said of it is, that a bad precedent has been set. There was no doubt as to who were in- strumental in carrying the proposition—it was the women, who formed DO small part of the andience, and judging by the enthusiasm with which they took up the proposition,one couldn't but think that they would have acted just the same had the pro- posed increase been one of Is, 31.

The flower carnival and lantern parade, pro- moted by the members of the Runcorn Cycle Club, and to be held next Wednesday promises to be a success. If the various cycle clubs in the dis- trict who have promised assistance, fulfil their obligations, there will be quite a picturesque medley on wheels. The tail end should prove most laughable for there is to be represented Muldoon's picnic party and the Darktown fire brigade. In addition to the cyclists,there will be a lifeboat exhibited, and other attractions. Three or four bands will also be in attendance. The pro- ceeds are to be devoted to two most worthy object one-half being for toe benefit of the Runcorn Dis- trict Nurse Fund, and the other half for the National lifeboat Institution. Neither of these institutions needs words of commendationoind it is to IN hoped that our local wheeler, will be encour- aged in their tiforLs by a li ;wet response by the public.

RUNCORN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIE ry.

ANNUAL MEETING.-LIVELY PRO CEEDINGS.

In the Public Hall, Runcorn. on Tuesday evening, the annual meeting of the Runcorn Co-operative Industrial and Provident Society, Limited, was held, and the pro- ceedings were characterised by a considerable amount of warmth. For over three hours there were animated dis- cussions on different matters relating to the society, and in which there was generally a crossing of swords by the Widnes and Runcorn members. The meeting was of a representative character, and the audience included a considerable number of women. The society consists of 3,996 members, and in submitting the half-yearly statement of accounts, a summary of which was given in our last issue, the comnaittee recommended a divi- dend of 2s. 91. in the 2, which would leave a balance of £3B 3s. 71(1 to be carried to the reserve fund. Mr. D. Worthington was voted to the chair, and he was accom- panied on the platform by Messrs. Holt, W. Rhodes, W. Cropper, J. Sayle, and J. ' Rogerson (members of the general committe,.), Mr. W. Upton (secretary), and Mr. Jonah Evans (manager).

THE FIRST BREEZE. All was plain soling until the minutes of a special meeting which was held at Widnes, came to be con- firmed. Mr. Watson rose, and stated that he wished to call the attention of the members to a contravention of the rules which had taken place. He read an extract from the rules in which it stated that any ten members could call a special meeting, and notice of the meeting, with the business to be transacted should be sent to each member within six days of such meeting. He contended that the fixing of the fees for the members of the com- mittee appointed to revise the rules of the society was not given notice of,and was therefore illegal.—The Chair- man said the previous speaker's criticism was fair and straightforward. If they could do one irregular thing at a special meeting they might do two, three, or four. But the question was, as the thing had been done, how could they get out of the difficulty ?—Mr. Watson hoped the members would bear in mind what had- been said, for he should move a resolution when the balance sheet came to be considered regarding the cost of the revision of rules.—Mr. Goodier considered that the Runcorn members bad not been treated fairly with regard to the special meeting at Widnes. They were entirely ignored. He proposed a resolution "That this meeting relieves and discharges from their duties all the members of the revising com- mittee appointed at the special meeting held at Widnes and that their amended rules be not accepted.--Mr. Dudley stated that he understood from the balancesheet, that a special meeting for the revision of rules was to be held at Ruricorn on the 20th August. He thought they were already holding that meeting.—The Chairman said the meeting could now alter, amend or rescind any of the minutes of the special meeting. If they were now confirmed they could not be altered afterwards.—Mr. Dudley said the revision committee thought it better to leave the revision of rules over until the special meeting. Each member would then be furnished with a copy of them, and they could then be fully considered. He pro- posed that the minutes of the sp-icial meeting be passed as read.—Mr. Wilkinson seconded the amendment.—Mr. Walter Worthington in order to test the feeling of the meeting seconded the proposition made by Mr. Goodier. —Mr. Moorea said if the rules were brought before the members and discussed at the Ispeeial meeting it would be far better. It was very wrong for Mr. Watson to propose that the rules be abolished before the members knew something of them.—Mr. Watson : I have not suggested anything of the kind.—The Chairman said Mr. Moores was the first to make a discourteous remark. Their society was a democratic institution, and every- thing was left in the hand of the members. He would now put the amendment to the meeting.—Mr. Dudley again rising, said the members did not know what they were asked to condemn.—A Voice : He has been on his feet three or four times.—The Chairman : You waist some seese before you speak.—A Member : The Chairman is taking one view of the case.—A Lady Member : What is it for ? (Laughter)—The Chairman : You ladies must not talk and chatter, we are here for business —(Laughter.)—On being put to the vote, a shower of hands went up both for the amendment and resolution and the Chairman said he did not know which had it.—Mr. Rogerson said he had kept his seat as long as he possibly could, and he must say that the members did not know what they were comdemning. They elected a certain number to do a certain work, and that had been done to the best ability of the men selected. As to the payment of the men in question, if the meeting had power to elect them to do their duty they had also power to fix the remuneration. The members did not even know what amended rules were until they were brought before them.—The discussion was then proceeded with without let or hindrance.—Mr. B. Kniveton in the course of a few remarks said it seemed to him that the meeting was in the dark concerning the rules. It was proposed to take power from the Runcorn members and plane it in the hands of the Widnes members. were they going to allow that ?—(" No ")—He should say a thousand times; no ; let them stick to their own. He had had the privilege of seeing what the voluntary com- mittee's eules were, and he thought they should he placed side hg side with the authorised committee's rules—Mr. Holt said he was responsible for the resolu- tion that the rules be reviemi. He was appointed on the committee. but he never intended the constitution of the encietv to be altered. He intended to stick to the society as organised, viz the Runcorn Co-operative Society. He knew there were several members of their own Society willing to pay five per cent, on the allotments system. RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

A meeting of this Board was held at Dutton Work- house on Tuesday afternoon. The chair was occupied by Mr. J. W. Knowles, and there were present in addi- tion to the members of the Runcorn Rural Council, Rev. Canon Wood, Rev. _Dr: Gastaldi, Mr. Thomas Handley,

J.P.' Mr. Varwood, Mr. A. R.' Norman. Mr. Ashton (clerk), and Mr. W. Hough (deputy clerk).

VAGRANCY.

In acknowledging the receipt of resolutions passed by the Guardians, at their meeting on the 23rd ult., Colonel Hamersley, chief constable of Cheshire, stated that the county police were vigilant in their endeavours to check vagrancy, and he would suggest for the consideration of the Board that if they desired the matter to receive the attention of the justices throughout the county, a copy of the resolution should be forwarded to each justices' clerk, with a request that the same be brought before the magistrates in each Petty Sessional division.—On the motiom of Mr. Mclntyre, seconded by the Rev. Dr. Gastaldi,it was decided to carry out the recommendation of Colonel Hamereley.

THE FRODiIIAM RELIEVING OFFICER. A letter was read from the Local Government Board in which they stated that they approved of the proposal to increase the salary of Mr. Riley, relieving officer of Fredsham, from £75 to £lOO.

SCHOOL ATTENDANCR OFFICER. Writing on the 29:h July, the Local Government Board approved of the proposal to increase the salary of Mr. F. A. Jenkins, school attendance offi.;er of the Bud- worth district to £35 per annum. They also approved of the appointment of Mr. W. B.Brimelow,as attendance officer, at a salary of £l5 per annum.

SMALLPDX.

The Local Government in an order respecting small pox in casual wards and workhouses, drew attention to a previous circular letter, stating that only circum- stances of grave urgency justified the admission or re- tention of a smallpox case in the Workhouse, but when such a case occurred the other inmates should be re- vaccinated, and the greatest precautions tkken for isolation. It was stated by Mr. Handley that the Visit- ing Committee had considered the regulations, and the Master had told them that they were carried out. This concluded the business.

ORMY'S CIRCUS, WARRINGTON.

MR. OHMY El) AT THE COUNTY COURT.

At the Warrirgton County Court, on Thursday, before his Houour Judge Wynne Ffoulkse, John Lewis. con- tractor, 11 Henry-st., Warrington, sued Joseph Ohmy, circus proprietor, for £B3 15s. for extras in the building of a circus in Queen-st., Warrington. There was also a counterclaim for £lOO for damages owing to the loss of a tt house," on E ester Monday afternoon, and for the loss of the services of several performing horses which it was alleged caught colds, owing to the state of the circus. Mr. Arthur Browne appeared on behalf of the plaintiff, , and Mr. Tobin, banister, instructed by Mr. H. F.Neale, Liverpool, represented the defendant. Mr. Browne, in opening the case, said that the claim which had been made would be modified to some extent and would be reduced to £72 18s. 10d., the mistake hay ing arisen owl .g to the figures being wrongly reckoned- Mr. Lewis happened to hear that Mr. 013 my wanted a circus building in Warrington. He met him in Man- chester and asked him if it was true. Mr. Ohmy said that it was, and Mr. Lewis said that he would like to tender for the erection of it. Mr. Ohmy told him that , be could see the plans of the circus deposited in the office of the Borough Surveyor of Warrington. The circus was to be built of corrugated iron according to , that plan. Mr. Ohmy said that there was a . wall seven feet high which was not to be covered with corrugated iron so as to save part of the cost. Thereupon, Mr. Lewis tendered for the building and took into consideration the wall which was not to be covered. There were no specifications or details in the plan, which would be produced in due course. Mr. Lewis sent in the tender on March 15, undertaking to complete the building ready for the proprietor by Easter Monday, for the sum of £294 103., erected as nearly as possible as per the plan submitted, any altera- tion to be considered as extras, and any deduction to be t!eated in a similar way. In case of any dispute, the borough surveyor was to act as arbitrator. Mr. Chrny met Mr. Lewis on the 19th March at the Manchester Central Station, and after discussing the matter, Mr. Ohmy accepted the tender for £291 103. Mr. Lewis, however, agreed to build it for £287 103. on the understanding that on the completion of the term for which Mr. Ohmy wanted the building for perform- ances, he would sell it to Mr. Lewis for the sum of £lOO.

Mr. Lewis then proceeded to carry out the contract. Mr. °luny said that he could not pay the money down, and an agreement was drawn up, whereby £5O was to be paid on the day of assignment, £5O on April 13, £27 10s. on April 21, and the balance £59 103. on May 13. Strange to say Mr. Ohmy kept the agreement in his pos- session. Mr. Lewis Commenced the work on Monday, March 20, and it had to b. completed by April 13. so that a large number of men had to be employed in order to put the structure ap in what was practically 23 days. On Monday March 20, Mr. Lewis pegged out the ground according to the plan, and he then found that it did not come near to the walls which Mr. Ohmy suggested it should do. He immediately telegraphed to Mr. 0 imy and that gentleman came over the next day, the work being stopped during that time. Mr. Ohm; then in pre- sence of two witnesses instructed Mr. Lewis to enlarge the circus to fill the full extent of the space, and thus made the building capable of holding 300 more persons than it would according to the original plan. Mr. Lewis proceeded with the work and had got as far as putting down the ring in the centre of the circus. The ring was pegged out according to plan when Mr. Ohmy came over and on noticing the ring he said that too much room had been given for the boxes,and that more room ought to be given to the gallery which paid the best. Mr. Lewis accordingly ale red the gallery from eight to twelve tiers and thus gave a large amount of extra accommedatien. In consequence of this very great enlargement ordered by Mr. Ohmy, it was almost impossible to get the circus aown in the time, but Mr. Lewis did everything hel I could do, and it Was a matter of astonishment to every- body, including Mr. Ohmy, that it was finished in time. In order to finish the building, it wee necessary to work all Good Friday, Saturday, and Easter Sunday morning, when it was ready to be put into the hands of the de- corator. The decorators did not then turn up, but it was not Mr. Lewis's fault. The circus was opened on I Monday night, and there was a very crowded house and a most successful performance. Mr. Ohmy made a speech at the opening in which he expressed his satis- tion that the bnilding had been completed in so short a time. It was open for nine weeks, and no complaint whatever was made during the whole of that time. Mr. Lewis was coestantly at the circus during performances, and was also in the rooms with the artistes, and was fre- qilently in company with Mr. Ohmy, and yet there was not a single complaint of any deseription,and never was until Mr. Lewis applied for the extras. The money for the contract had been paid in instalments as agreed upon' and on May 13, Mr. Lewis sent in his bill for ex- tras, but Mr. Ohmy declined to pay any extras. The matter was then placed in his (Mr. Browne') hands,and Mr. Ohmy still refused to pay. With regard to the Icounter-claim, he (Mr. Browne) would prove that no I performance was advertised for Monday afterneon, and therefore there was no loss on that account. The first performance was advertised for Monday night, and this performance actually came off. Mr. Ohmy had a splen- did record during the whole of the time he was in Warrington, and only three weeks ago the building was used for a political meeting at which there were 2,500 persons present. The building was not in the slightest degree worse than it was before. If it allowed water to come throuels and also caused seve- ral horses to have colds, Mr. Ohmy had no right tc have performances, nor should the borough surveyor have allowed the building to be used. He, however, con- tended that the building was well fitted for the purpose for which it was intended. Mr. Ohmy had reckoned £33 13s. for the loss he had sustained, owing to there being no performance on Monday afternoon. Even allowing that a performance had been advertised, he (Mr. Browne) contended that the figure claimed Was too high. There had been two benefit nights in the circus, one for Mrs. Sheridan, at which the is. 6d. seats were charged 2s , and at which there was a full house, and vet the total receipts had only been £2B 19,. 61. He -(Mr. Browne) who had assisted in getting up the ar- rangements, was present at the time the money was counted. There had also been a benefit for the Infirmary and this had realised £29 10i. 61 , which Mr. Ohnw made into £3O. It was, therefore,ahsurd,he contended, to claim £33. Mr. Browne then mentioned the names of several horses which it was alleged had taken colds owing to the state of the circus. He contended that they had rot taken cold owing to the circus,but if they had taken cold, it was likely they had caught it during a long journey from Oldham. As a matter of fact one horse was performing during the whole of the time Mr. ()limy was in Warrine.am.

_ Mr. Browne durincz the course of the case. amended his elaim by adding £9 for wood used to carry the water from the ceiling over the wall.

John Lewis, the plaintiff, was then called and gave evidence corroborative of Mr. Browne' s statement. He said that the circus was ten or twelve feet longer, and three feet wider than it was to be according to the original plan. The building was finished by Sunday afternoon, but the decorators could have been at their work on the Wednesday previously. But for the enlarge- ment which Mr. Ohmy required, the building vould have been finished three days previously. He never heard about any afternoon performance being contemplated until the counter-claim was made. He produced the Warrington "Examiner" in confirmation of this. Mr. Ohmy never made any complaint to him about the circus, although he was frequently in his company, in fact Mr. Ohmy made a speech expressing surprise that the building had been completed so soon. As far as he (Mr. Lewis) was concerned, there might have been an afternoon performance.—Cross-examined : He had never built a eiccus before, but he had told Mr. Ohmy that he had experience in erecting similar structures, as he had built most of the temporary buildings on the Manchester Ship Canal. He never mentioned anything about pay- ment for the extras at the time that he was building the circus. He understood that the circtis was to be up for 13 weeks. In the circus, daylight could b seen in places, but the openings were net of any consequence. He had been e the circus one wet night, hut water did not pour in.—Re-examined : Where the daylight could be seen, it was where the old wall joined the eaves. At this point, Mr. Tobin suggested that as the case would probably last two or three days, it would save the time of the court if Mr. Lewis and Mr. Ohmy went before an arbitrator nominated by his Honour, and the interests of justice would thereby be best served. He would also suggest that neither lawyers nor witnesses be Present but that the arbitrator go over the building with two parties. Mr. Browne agreed to this, and eventually Mr. William Owen, li'., was selected, Mr. Browne also agreed to forego the £9 with which the claim had been amended. Mr. William Owen was then sent for, and agreed to accept the position of arbitrator. '

THE IRISH PARTY DISSENSIONS.

MANIFE3rO BY MR. JUSTIN MTARTHY.

The "Freeman's Journal" publishes a manifesto to the Irish people from Mr. Justin Wearthy, which deals in the main with the results of the late general election and the causes which led to those results. Mr.M'Carthy puts in the front of these the disunion amongst the ' members of the Irish party and Mr. Healv's conduct at the Omagh Convention. After recapitulating what took place there,he says it is with deep regret that he is com- pelled to characterise Mr. Healy's conduct as disloyal to the party, and,setting aside their own special obligation to each other,as a breach of the ordinary code of honour and discipline essential to the existence of every politi- cal organisation. "The Omagh scandal," he proceeds, " is unhappily the latest of a series of attacks on the unity and efEniency-ot the Irish party. Let us endea- vour it shall he the last. A repetition must bring ruin on the cause." He leAs bound, on the eve of the open- ing of Parliament,to addresi a respectful word of warn- ing to his fellow-comv rymen and to inform his collea- gues of the views he holds in case he is again elected to the chair.

DISPUTE ABOUT A KITCHEN RANGE.

CASE AT THE WARRINGTON COUNTY COURT. An action was heard at the Warrington County Court on Thursday before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, in which Messrs.Williarns and Clay,monumental masons, &c., Bank Quay, sued Enoch William Sankey, a farmer, and large property owner at Croft, for £B, value of a ' kitchen range supplied to him by the plaintiffs on June ' 20. Mr. W. J. Forshaw appeared an behalf of the plain- tiffs, and Mr. Fowler for the defendant. Mr. Forshaw said that the defendant visited the business premises of the plaintiffs and asked to be shown some kitchen ranges. Mr. George Clay, a partner in the plaintiffs' firm, showed him two or three ranges, and named the prices. After selecting one, defendant said that he was not quite sure whether a right or a left hand range was required, but that his wife would know. The plaintiffs accordingly caused a telephonic message to be sent to the defendant's wife, who replied that a right hand grate was required. The defendant then bargained with Mr. Clay as to the price of the grate selected, end eventually agreed to pay a 103. casteor £7 credit. Two or three hours afterwards a lucre, was sent frem defend-1 ant's farm to take the range away. On the following Tuesday the range was brought back by the same man who took it away. He was asked for an explanation, but could not give a satisfactory one, except that Mr. Sankey intended calling upon the plaintiffs about the matter very shortly. As the defendant did not call, an invoice was sent to him hy the plaintiffs, who warned him that the range was lying at the premises of the plaintiffs at his own risk, and asking him to bring it away at once, and remit a cheque for the same. No reply was received to this communication, and a letter was sent saying that legal proceedings would be taken if a cheque was not sent. This did not receive an answer and the plaintiff then placed the matter into the hands of Messrs. Davies and Forshaw. He (Mr. Forshaw) caused the usual letter to be sent to the defen- dant but to this also 120 reply had been sect.—Mr. George Clay corroborated his solicitor's statement. He said that when the range was returned, it was very rusty, and had depreciated in value.—The judge : But you are sueing for the price are you not 2—Mr. Clay : We are, your Honour.—ln reply to Mr. Fowler, witness denied that he would have taken the range back without ques- tion, if it had been in the same state as it was when sent out. The plaintiffs might have allowed the defendant to have made another selection. He did not send the grate on approval, nor did he say anything to the defendant to the effect that the range would be taken back if not approved of by defeodant's wife; neither did he promise to get the defendant another, if that grate did not suit. He also contradicted the statement made by the lurry- man that the grate would be taken back on the payment of 103. for the trouble of taking it up and down the stairs. A message was sent to the defendant's house to see Mr. Sankey about the grate but he was not instructed to ask for another order for a grate.—Thomas aim of the firm of Williams and Clay, gave similar evid- ence.—William Walter Westwell, coal merchant, spoke ,to telephoning ta the defendant's wife as to the kind of grate required, and that she replied that a right hand grate was required.—Walter Adnaki Clay, son of Mr. George Clay spoke to going over to Mr. Sankey'a house on his bicycle to see him about the grate. He was surprised when he got to the house to find that another kitchen range had been erected.—Mr. Fowler contended that the grate was simply sent on approval. His client at the time he visited the place of the plaintiff, had clearly told them that if he did not care for the grate he would send it back. He was also instrected that when the man took the grate back, Mr. Williams told him that he would re- quire 103. for the taking of it up and down stairs.— Enoch William Senkey corroborated Mr. Fowler's state- ment, and said that the reason why he did not reply to the letters sent to him was because he considered them to be impertinent. Ralph Saunderson, carter in the em- ploy of Mr. Sankey, stated that when he took back the grate, Mr. Williams told him that he would require 10s. for taking down the grate—ills Honour Raid that the plaintiffs had the right to insist upon their bargain. Assuming that the grate was sent on approval, it seemed idle to say that five days would be required to prove it. In fact, the wife had shown by getting the other grate that e ,ly 24 hours were required. He would therefore give judgment for plaintiffs._

SHOCKING FATALITY ON THE SHIP

CANAL. TWO MEN KILLED.

A disastrous accident, unhappily resulting in the death of two men and injuries to several ethers, occurred on Thursday on the Ship Canal at Latchford. It will be remembered that on the 10th April last the gates of the large lock, which is wort. long by 65ft. wide, were car- ried away by the steamship Harold, one falling on the sill of the lock and the other hanging to the wall. The fallen gate was as speedily as possible removed to a position in the canal where it would not impede the navigation, and the other placed on a piece of land near the locks. Within the last few days a gang of men have been engaged in the task of drawing the submerged gate from the waterway and placing it upon dry land. Derricks and winches were fixed, and a set of wire ropes of great strength, four in number, were attached to the gate by means of slings. The actual work of hauling up the mass of wood and iron, which is said to weigh upwards of 120 tons, was commenced on Monday morning, and good progeess was made with what was a most difficult undertaking, and one which required the exercise of great skill and patience. By three o'clock on Thursday afternoon the gate had been drawn practically clear of the water,and occupied a slanting position over the land at the side of the canal, on which it was proposed to place it. About thirty men were engaged in the work, Mr. W. H. Hunter,chief engineer to the Canal Company, and Mr. Fox, the resident mechanical engineer, being present. At about half-past three it Lecame necessary to remove one of the ropes to a point at the other end of the gate, in order to bring it into a horizontal position preparatory to the final effort of drawing it up. Some of the men were at this moment on the gate,and a number of others were hauling on the slack of one of the wire cables which, to the length of some 30 or 40 yards, ran out from the derrick over the winch. A terrible result en- sued in the attempt to change the position ot the cable. The rope next to it gave way at the ling, and part of the tackle at the other end of the gate also snapped. The gate canted over, and that portion of the cable on which the men were hauling was instantaneously tightened to s' raining point. The bracing up of the hitherto slack rope was so sudden, and the force which tightened it so tremendous, that the men were thrown into the air with great violence. A man named Edward Whitlow, about 34 years of age, who had apparently been stoop- ing over the cable at the mment of the accident, was found to have sustained shocking injuries ; his body being, in fact, nearly severed, An elderly man named John Parnell was also so badly hurt that death ensued almost immediately. The foreman, a man named Michael Scully, was injured about the legs, and other of the men were more or less bruised end cut. The accid- ent caused the utmost consternation, Latchford and the neighbourhood being in a ferment of excitement. Mr. Marshall, the lock master, at once, upon one of the Com- pany's locomotives, went to Knutsforclroad and tele- phoned to Warrington for medical aid, and very shortly Dr. Parker, of Stockton Heath, and other medical men from Warrington were on the spot, and rendered what assistance was necessary to those who had been injured. The bodies of Whitlow and Parnell were placed in an outbuilding near the scene of the accident, where they will remain till the coroner's inquest has been held. Whitlow leaves a widow and two children. He is a , native of the place, and has been frequently employed by the Canal Company. Parnell, wh ) was known among the workmen by the name of" General," has also been employed about the canal almost from the commencement of its construction. Later in the after- noon the gate was secured in its position, and it is ex- pected that an attempt will be made to complete the work of removal at once, had the operations proceeded without accident, the whale would, it is said, have been completed in about an hour. The tackle employed was of the best quality, and had been subjected to severe tests before it was used.

WIDNES FOOTBALL CLUB.

£5OO TO HE SPENT.

A special meeting of the committee of the Widnes Football Club, was held on Friday night, when the ten- ders for the laying out of their new field were submitted. The Committee decided to accent Mr. T. Sadler's tender, whose price was close upon £5OO, and the ground will be laid out according to the plan draws up by Mr. T. F. Sadler. The field rented by the club is on the south side of the cricket ground, and is 210 yards by 160. On the south side will be erected a vimmodious stand, and under this the players will be provided with a pavilion and dressing-room. The goal posts will face east and west, while the ground on the north side will be banked up. If nr-cessary the enclosure could be made to accom- modate 20,000 persons. It is the Committee's intention to make a portion of the ground into a field for practice. Inside the spectators' rin,,,which will be hoarded round, instead of a single iron rail being used, provision is made for a track, 18 feet wide, SO that in future yesrs there will he no necessity for the Athletic Club to hold their festival at Farnworth. There will be three turnstiles in Lowerhouse-lane, and two exit gates. The work will be commenced this week, and Mr. Sadler, it is said, is under penalty of £lO per week, if the work is not finished in five weeks from the day he signs the agreement.

SHIP CANAL, AUSTRALIAN MUTTON FOR MANCHESTER._

The ship Timaru is due to arrive at Manchester from Australia about the end of the present month. She brings about 16,000 carcases of sheep. This is the first direct consignment of fr7z.n meat to Manchester, and much interest is felt in the enterprise, as further ship- ments are certain in the event of its success.

STRANGE OCCURRENCE IN A LIVE RPOOL HOTEL.

GAS POISONING.

A young girl named Eleanor Wood. who was staying with her father overnight at a temperance hotel in Man- chester-et., Liverpool,was found unconscious on Monday morning, and was taken in the horse ambulance to the Southern Hospital. It was her intention to proceed in the afternoon to America, and her father had come down from Norfolk to see her off. She was in a very precarious condition, and on arrival at the hospital it was discovered that the girl was suffering from gas poisoning. When a police constable was called to the house a strong smell of gas was noticeable in the room which the girl occupied, and it is presumi-d that Miss Wood either neglected to turn the gas off properly oe coining as she did from a country place, and being un— accustomed to gas, blew it out before retiring.

ALLEGED EXTENSIVE THEFTS AT

HALTON.

The interior of Runcorn Police Court presented an un- usual appearance on Thursday morning, and looked fo the time being, to have been converted in a genera storehouse. There were boxes upon boxes of goods ap- pertaining to a general dealer, and if catalogued were sufficient to make a formidable list. The goods had been brought to the police station from the house of William and Ann Travers, of Halton, and that they were numer- ous may be known from the fact that there were two. shandry loads full. It appears that Ann Travers, who has for a great number of years resided at Halton, has been employed by Messrs. Gamen and Rowe, tea and general dealers, Hal- ton, as ch,rwoman, but nothing was suspected of her conduct until a fortnight ago, when from some information received, P.C. Juett watched her, and from what took place on Wednesday afternoon, he arrested her and brought her to the police station. Her husband, who was employed at the Runcorn Gas Works, was also arrested. They were both charged on Thursday morning before Mr. Thomas Handley, with stealing nine pounds of flour, eleven eggs, a dozen boxes of matches and other things, the property of Messrs. Gamon and Rowe. of Halton, and with receiving them' knowing them to be stolen. They made no reply. The evidence adduced was merely for the purpose of obtaining an order for the remand of the prisoners.—Superintendent Atherton stated that the female prisoner had been employed by Messrs. Gamon and Rowe as charwoman,end her duty on Wednesday afternoon, when the shop was closed for the half holiday, was to clean out the shots. She had abumd the confidence reposed in her, and had clandestinely removed goods, in bags which she had made to tie round her waist. A watch had been kept by P.C. Juett rid he caught her the previous day taking some goods away in the manner mentioned.—P.C. Juett stated that he saw the female prisoner coming from Messrs. Gamon and Itowe's shop, the previous day. H. beckoned her to sop but she went on to her house which was about thirty yards away. He followed her, and afterwards got his wife to search her. The prisoner how- ever so resisted hi a wife that he bad to obtain the assist- ance of another woman. The bags produced were found tied round the prisoner's body, and the goods men- tioned in the charge, with others, were found in them. He brought her to the Police Station and charged her with the offence,but she made no reply. He afterwards went hack to the house and found a large quantity of goods, 9ornq of u hich were stored under the beds. The female prisoner had been employed by them for about 25 years.—lt was stated in court :hat the prisoners had a banking account of over £3oo.—They were remanded until two o'clock this(Friday) afternoon.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

CHARGE OF NEGLECTING • DAUGHTER. At the W arrington Borough Court, on Friday, before Dr. Gornall and Mr. S. Wright, Jane Norman, 4, Bay Horse Yard, was charged with neglecting her daughter Jane Norman, on July 29. Inspector McAteer, N sy.c.c. Said that the defendant was the wife of Edward Norman, at present in the Workhouse. She lodged with Mrs. Thempson, and had tt•ree children. The girl Jane, was 15 years of age, and was an apprentice fustian cutter to Mr. Wilkinson. Up to the last three or four weeks she had been constantly at her work, but the last three weeks she had been unable to go to her work for the want of proper clothing and other wearing apparel, but the mother turned her out day by day. As a consequ- ence the girl had to sleep in the tramp ward of the Work- house night after night. On July 29, witness went to see the girl in the tramp ward, and found her in a very filthy condition. Be atterwarde went to the house of her mother and found Mrs. Norman under the influence of drink. The room in which the girl had to sleep was almost destitute of furniture. Evidence was given by Jane Norman, Mrs. Thompson and Detective Monaghan, and Inspector McAteer said that if the defendant agreed to the girl being sent to a home which Mr. Taylor,?. C. M.. would fiod her.he would consent to the case being with- drawn. Mrs. Norman having consented to this course, she was discharg,ed. A NEWSPAPER BOY IN TROUBLE. At the Warrington Borough Court on Wednesdav,he- fore Mr. J. Fairclough, Mr. J. Geddes, and Mr. J. W. Hayward, John William Bate, a boy of 14 years of age, was charged with stealing by meads of a trick, from thel office of the" Liverpool Weekly Post," and " Echo," 17, I Winwick-st., on Monday afternoon, eight dozen " Echos," and one penny in cash. The Chief Constable said that the prosecutors were represented by Mr. Charles Lyon. The defendant was the son of Henry Bate, forge labourer, 363, Winwick-rd. About 2.30 on Monday afternoon, prisoner along with a number of other boys were in the office of the" Liverpool Post," and prisoner was supplied with his" Echos " first, because he had a parcel to take from the office to the 'bus which goes to Stocktnn Heath. The parcel contained eight dozen " Echos," and were valued at 2s. Bd. A penny was given to the prisoner to pay for the carriage of the par- cel to Stockten Heath. The parcel was addressed to James Whitley, who resides at that place. Mr. Whit- ley met the 'bus as usual at Stockton Heath, but he could ret find the parcel of " Echos." 'The matter be- came known to the parties at the " Echo" office, and later ,n in the evening at about eight o'clock a man named Thomas John Gordon, met the prisoner who had sold foer 7.2 G papers, and still had three dozen left. tie took possession of them, and the matter was reported later on by Mr. Lyon to Detective Monaghan who made a search far the boy, but did not find him until midnight on Tuesday. He was brought drown to the police station, and when charged ho pleaded guilty—Mr. Lyon said that he did not wish the boy to suffer any imprisonment, and suggested a few strokes of the birch rod.—The magistrates however, were in favour of sending him to a home or training ship, and the case was remanded until Saturday for Inquiries to be made as to a suitable place to send him.

Evidence was given by Mr. Worthington, Frank Metcalf, and Edward Repson, and. the bench at that point said that they did not think the Case need go any further, as they were of opinion that the parties were "six of one and half a dozen of the other," and they would dismiss the case, each side to pay its own costs. CRMICET.

The Warrington team had a holiday fixture on Mon- day, and a hcliday on Siturday. The team they met on Monday was Llandudno and a pretty mess they made of the bowling averages of someof the Warrington bowlers. For the loss of only five wickets. Llandudno scored 184 runs, or an average of almost 37 par wicket. No fewer than seven Warrington bowlers were tried and the most successful was J. J. Cro.sfi,ld whose two wickets cost 48 runs, Bates, G. Clarke, and E. Woods. each took a wicket, but Callon, Bibby and Mortimer could get never a wicket.

The fielding of the Warrington men was not so good as is usually th,. case. Had the match teen played to a finish. it is difficult to say how it would have resulted, but Warrington are enjoying an immunity from defeat which is rare in their recent history. They have not suffered defeat since the Liverpool match on June Bth, and their present record for the season is six wins, fivedraws, four lossos, and one (Broughton) abandoned. To have more wins than losses is quite a new experience to the club,but they will not be wise to shout until they are out of the wood.

As Warrington did not bat on Saturday, owing to the downfall of rain, the batting averages suffer no change this week, and consequently there is so necessity to publish them a.r.ain. Warrington have a rare chance to-clay of carrying off a victory. Until they met Oldham on June 15th,thpv had only gained a solitary victory,but they quite B'll-prised everybody by inflicting a crushing defeat upon the "Rugh yetis," and have never looked hack since. They ought to see that they do not allow Oldham to surprise them in a like fashion. The Oldham cricketers are not quite like the Oldham footballers, but they are not to be despised for all that.

The C.L.C. Engineers revenged the defeat they sus- tained some time ago at the hands of the Sankey Wes- leyans in fine style on Stturday. The way they knocked over the Wesleyans' wickets was a sight to see, and when the summing up came, it was found that the latter had only nut together 9 runs ! The top scorer was Light with 4, and there were two extras, so that the remaining 10 bud not much to boast about. All but Clare were clear bowled, and he was run out. The bowling analyses of Harrison load Monks, the C.L.C. bowlers, are worth publishing. Harrison bowled five overs, and out of these, four were maidens, and he took five wickets for one run, while Monks took four wickets for six runs. On going in to bat, the Engineers did none too avel,for th,. first two each contributed a"duck," hut for all that 49 was scored. Llewellyn was the most successful Wesleyan bowler for he took five wickets, Walker alone scored more than twice the Wesleyans' total. On the whole, the match is not one of which the West Enders have much to boast.

The second team also had to knuckle under to r.hA Engineers second on Saturday, and that was by 78 to 17. In this case, however, the Wesleyans had not their best team on, and were thus conslderably handicapped.

The Penketh Wesleyans have been busy during the holidays,for they had matches on Saturday and Monday, and won both. The match with Rough Green was de- cisive enough being 58 to 27, but that with the ser- geants of Orford B4rracks was still more so,as the latter scored 17 to Penketh 104. It must, however, be men- tioned that the sergeants were four men short, and had to play subs. The Penketh team ought to be well satis- fied with the two matches, for besides winning both, they have scored 162 rens to their opponents 44. ---- Cairo-st. Unity paid a visit to the beautiful district of Arley on Monday, and engaged the team of that name. They had, however, to return home defeated by 78 to 48. Woods and Edes were the best scorers for the Unity. Smith, the Arley secretary, was the top scorer of the match and has 16 opposite his name.

Things have been very quiet in first slass cricket. The matches which commenced on Wink holiday have all been very much hindered by rain, and in two cases, Lancashire v. Yorkshire and Gloucester and Susspx. whole day actually passed without a ball being bowkri The interest in the statistical table of the first class counties is fast waning, tor now there does not appear to be much chance of any other county taking the hlare of Surrey at the top of the table. The only remaining interest in the table is as to the order the teams will be. The first and last, Susrey and Somerset, appear to be tolerably safe for their positions, but then there is plenty of room for change in the others..

The annual August Bank Holiday fixture between Lancashire and Yorkshire ended in a draw, and both counties have each won 23 matches, so that next year they will have a fresh start. The match this week was very ctiaegeable. In the first innhags of the Lancashire team, the batsmen failed completely, and Yorkshire had no diffieulty in passing the score and putting on a large number of runs in excess. They applied the closure and left Lancashire 167 to get to avert a single innings defeat, hut so steady did Lancashire play that this feat was accomplished and Yorkshire were robbed of vic- tory.

Surrey have not had their umal luck this week. and were robbed by the rain of a certain victory over Notts. However they hive had so much good fortune thia season that they ought not to grumble at a little bad luck.

SAD DROWNING FATALITY AT BEWSEY.

On Friday evening, a boy named James Edward Griffiths. four years of age, son of Henry Griffiths, lead- raster. 2, Ellis-street, Bewsey-road, Warrington, was playing with some other children at the turn bridge over the St. fle!ens canal, at Lodge-lane, Bewsry, when in turning a somersault on the bridge, he fell into the water. His companions at once raised the alarm, and a man employed on the Canal a short time afterwards pulled the boy out of the water, but too late to save his life. Mr. F. A. Jones. deputy c-roner, held an inquest on the body of the deceased on Tuesday, at the Imperial Hotel, Bewsey-road, Mr. Mitchell was th- fareman of the jury. .1 he father of the deceased ssid that his son left home about 715 on Friday night. The cana was about half a mile from his house. The deceased had previously been warned not to go near to the canal. Fred Forster, one of the boys who were playing with the deceased at the time of the accident, said that the deceased was try- ing to toss a somersault over the rails of the bridge and missed his hold and fell into the water. Thomas Mills, engine driver rm a dredger, said that he was near the bridge on Friday night at the time the accident occurred. Immediately upon hearing of the boy being in the water, witness commenced to search for the body, and it was recovered about eight o'clock. In reply to questions by the jury, the witn.ss said that the rails on the bridge were about a foot with, and boys could awing through them. The Coroner said that the more danger there was, the more boys wc.,uld get into it. If the jurors desired they could make a presentment which he would forward to the oroper quarters. One of the jurymen mentioned that shoot. thief+ weeks ago, the scholars in the Ragged School were spending a day in a field near to the bridge, and many of them flocked to the bridge. It was a won- der some of them were not drowned. After some con; siderable discussion, a verdict of " Accidental death " was returned, with a suggestion that better provision be made to prevent similar accidents in the future.

A CURIOUS 'NESTING PLACE.

Of all places for a bird's nest the buffer of a railway wagon surely beats the record. As a wagon laden with ironwork for a new jam factory was being recently un- loaded, one of the meu observed a little moss protruding from a small hole in the centre of one of the buffers. Upon examination he found inside the buffer a snug nest, con' aining one tiny egg. As the wagon had tra- velled from North Safford to Histon. near Cambridge, a distance ot 150 miles, the marvel is that the egg was not broken. The poet Cowper has immortalised a pair of chaffinches which built their nest in a pull y block near to the top of a mast, and then followed the ship to sea, and the above is perhaps equally deserving ef record.

One curious fact brought out by the report of public prosecutions in England is that the number of prosecu- ti,•na for murder in 1894 was identically the same as in 1893, sixty-two men and fifty-two wornm having been proceeded against in each year.

MR. GLADSTONE ON TFEE ARMENIAN QUESTION.

Mr. Gladstone addressed a great meeting on Tuesday at Chester on the subject of the outrages in Armenia. He moved a resolution assuring Her Majesty's Govern- ment that they will have the cordial support of the en- tire nation, without distinction of party, in any mea- sures which they may adopt for securing to the people of Turkish Armenia such reforms in the administration of that province as shall provide effective guarantees for the safety of life, honour, religion, and property. The resolution also declared that no reforms can be effective which are not placed under the continuous control of the Great Powers of Europe. Mr. Gladstone said this was no party or religious question. The statements of horrors which made the nation's blood run cold had been verified in all their sickening details, not only bY European testimony but by that of Americans. who, having no political inter- ests in Turkey, were witnesses doubly entitled to credit. The proceedings in Armenia might, he thought ; be summed up in four words—plunder,murder,rape,and ; torture. Plunder and murder, one would think, were had enough; but plunder and murder were almost venial by the side of the work of the ravisher and the work of the torturer as it was now fullyand authentically known to have been going on. ne held that the Turkish Govern- ment were morally responsibile for all the horrid deeds that had rec-ntly been put before the country. Mr. Gladstone pointed out that England in particular had power to redress these wrongs, and he insisted that the Ottoman Government should no longer be allowed to ignore its treaty obligations and promises. Every motive o, duty, he urged, coincided with every motive of self- respect in furtherance of this view. In replying to a vote of thanks, the rihit hon. gentleman said he had the firm conviction that this effort made by the people of Chester—an effort which might possibly be imitated In other places—would he viewed with a very friendly eye by Her Majesty's Government, and would tend to assist and net to impede them in the discharge of the grave and heavy responsibility which rested upon them , with respect to Armenia.

THE WESLEYAN CONFERENCE.

TEIE RACE QUESTION.

At Wednesday's sitting oithe Wesleyan Conference, resolutions were passed disapproving of proposals made by the South African Cfmforence as not being in harmony with the principles of Methodism touching fellowship and discipline, and declaring that the constitution of Methodism did not recognise in respect of the conditions of church membership any diversity of administration as between persons of different race and colour. The Conference decided that in indicating degrees possessed ty ministers the names of the universities conferring them should be entered in all official documents, but that the desree of DD. should be exempt from this role. The final reading of the stations was completed last evening.

===== 0002601_18950810_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
FRIENDLY SOCIETY MEMINGS.

FORESTER 3 AT BRIGHTON.

The sixtv-first annual High Court meeting of the Ancient Order of Foresters opened at Brighton on Mon- day. Brother George Allbery, High Chief Ranger, in his address, reviewed the work of the past year, and said it was highly gratifying to find that the efforts made to improve their financial position had resulted in an in- crease of capital during the twelve months of over a quarter of a million sterling—the largest increase ever recorded. Alluding to cld age pensions, he said that, I whatever may be attempted by ethers, it behoved them to offer every possible inducement and facility to their members to make provision for themselves. In his opinion the ultimate solution of the difficulty would be found in the cessation of sickness benefits and the com- mencement of an annuity at a given age. If they would retain their titles of self-reliant and self-dependent, and, what was of equal if not greater importance, if they wished to retain the absolute right to control and manage their own "'Affairs without interference from others, they must exercise all the energy they possessed and make all the necessary sacrifices to keep themselves from accepting gratuitous assistance from anybedv party whatsoever outside of their own mutual brother- hood.

The Foresters' High Court meeting was resumed at Brighton on Tuesday. Before the commencement of the ordinary business a resolution of sympathy with the sufferers from the fireworks accident at Preston Park on the previous evening was passed, and £lOO was voted on their behalf. It was decided that the High Court meeting of 1897 should be held at Norwich. The report of the Special Committee appointed a year ago to in- quire into the system of government of the Order was presented and discussed. The report proposed an entire alteration in the whole system of management, but each clause of it was rejected by an overwhelming majority. At Wednesday's meeting of the F)resters' High Court, permission was given to American subsidiary high courts to amplify the ritual of the Order for their own use. Two delegates crossed the Atlantic expressly to make this re- quest, and one of them stated that the ritual in the United States must be more elaborate than at present if they were to make any progress at all there. A long dis- cussion took place on a motion that the Executive Council should take steps to compel all courts having a relative degree of solvency of 17a. 6d. or less in the pound to take immediate action to place themselves in a more solvent position. Finall the resolution was lose by 188 votes to 148.

===== 0002601_18950810_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
INDEPENDENT ORDER OF RECHABITES.

The thirty-sixth High Movable Conference of the In- dependent Order of Reehabites took place at the Royal Pavilion, Brighton, on Tuesday. The report showed that the sickness and mortality returns for 1894 were favourable compared with previous years. The aver- age sickness per member was five and three-quarter days and the rate of mortality seven per thousand. The High Chief Ruler alluded in his address to the old-age pensioa question. Members of friendly societies must, he said, be careful not to allow State interference and control over the societies, which would have a tendency to undermine the self-reliance of the people and cause them to depend on the State for what they themselves were able to provide. At the Rechabites' meeting on Wednesday the official valuer presented his report, which showed that many tents in the Order are working on a table of contribu- tions which is not sufficiently high to ensure solvency. The report called forth considerable discussion. A strong determination was expressed in favour of all the tents being brought up to the point of absolute solvency, and the Board of Directors were authorised to suspend tents which fail to adopt adequ rte tables of contributions. It was further decided that Nelson's tables should be adopted throughout the Order for all new members.

ROYAL PRINCE'S IN DANGER.

STARTLING DISCOVERY AT PORTSMOUTH.

A Portsmouth correspondent states that the torpedo- boat destroyer Charger, upon which the Prince of Wales and the Duke of York had a trip to the Needles on Thursday last, was oaths following day to have her gun trials. These were, however, postponed, and for a singu- lar reason. It on Wednesday transpired that about two minutes after the Prince and tho Duke, who were ac- companied by several distinguished officers, landed at Portsmouth on Thursday, the attention of the engineer- ing staff was directed to the peculiar manner in which smoke was issuing from the Charger, and they found then that the crown of the fire-box was cracked right across. The fire was immediately drawn to prevent the boiler from bursting, and there the incident ended, but It is alleged that bad the crack occurred while the vessel was steaming twenty-seven knots a hour the result would probably have been a shocking disaster,

A MIDNIGHT ROW IN BATTERSBY LANE.

PLUCKY CONDUCT OF A SERGEANT.

At the Warrington Police Court, on Thursday, before Mr. S. Chandley and Colonel Ridgway, William Broad- bent, Forster-at., Ernest Priestley, and Fred Tilley, last from Present, were charged with being drunk and dis- orderly in Battersby-lane at 11.10 the previous evening. j They were also charged with assaulting and beating William Jenks, and Sergeant Oscar Stacey at the same time and place. The Chief Constable said that sometime on Wednes- day morning the three defendants were met by Sergeant Cheeney in Sankey-street,and they then wanted to know the way to Haydock-at., and another place. He directed them thither, and one of them then remarked that per- haps they would see him again. At about 11.10 the same evening, Mr. Jenks, who is the landlord of the Albion Hotel, Battersby-lane, unloosed his dog as was usual,and soon afterwards he heard it barking furiously. He went out and heard a scuffling outside the gate. Upon looking over he saw the three defendants scuffling, and on asking them what they were doing, he received a severe blow in the head with a stick in reply. Mr. Jenks then went out and followed the men and blew a whistle for help. The defendants then turned upon Mr. Jenks, and Sergeant Stacey, a non-commissioned officer, who resides in Lairs-st. came to his assistance and also several other men including P.C. Monks (38), who re- sides near to, and after great difficulty the defendants were brought to the police station. Sergeant Stacey was very badly injured in the struggle. William Jenks said that his dog commenced barking very savagely at about 11.10, and he thought some- thing must be wrong. He went into the yard, and stood on the cross bar of his back gate, and there saw the three defendants wrestling outside, all three being on the floor. Witne.rs said that they would be better at home, when Priestley got the stick produced, and broke it across his (witness's) head. Tilley also got the stick, and gave him a blow behind the ear, the force of the blow knocking him off the gate. Witness was not going to submit to this treatment, and he followed de- fendants to Laira-street. When they saw he was so close to them, they turned round on him, and Priestley and Tilley knocked him to the ground. Witness blew a policemiin's whistle, and Sergeant Stacey and others came to his assistance. Sergeant Stacey was severely kicked by one of them. He knew that Priestley bit him first with the stick, because of his bald head. Sergeant Stacey. who appeared in court with his nose severely lacerated, said that upon hearing the whistle blowing, he went out to see what was the matter and saw the last witness upon the ground, and two defend- ants assaulting !aim, Broadbent meanwhile standing by. IVitness shouted out " Stop that," but Priestley and Broadbent rushed at him and knocked him to the ground. While down on the ground, Tilley kicked him in the face, but other people coming up, the defendants were taken into custody. The kick which witness had received in the face had severely injured him, and five stitches had had to be put into his nose. The defend- ants were drunk at the time but still they knew what they were doing. Mr. Whitfield, who resides next door to the Albion, said that whets the defendants came to the back gate, they kicked at it bard enough to knock it dawn. He saw them bit Mr. Jenks with the stick, and he also followed them. One of them would have assaulted him but owing to help coming, he escaped. P.C. Monks said that he left duty at 10 o'clock on Wednesday evening,and was just going to bed, when he heard a whistle blow. He resided near to in Forshaw-st. He found the defendants drunk, and they had assaulted Mr. Jenks and Sergeant Stacy. He brought them to the Police Station with the assistance of several other per- sons. The defendants were not so drunk as Etat to know what they were doing. P.C. Cheney said that on Wednesday morning the de- fendants asked him the way to Haydock and Church streets. Re directed them, and then jocularly asked them if they wanted to know the way to anywhere else. They asked him if he could tell them the way to the Bridewell—(laughter)—and be told them that if they would go round by Church-st. they would get there.— Cress-examined by Priestley : He did not think that the defendants were serious.

The defendants expressed themselves as very sorry for what had been done, and said that they were drunk at the time,and had no clear recollection of what had taken place. The Bench decided to fine each defendant 30e. and costs or a month's imprisonment in default. They also complimented Sergeant Stacey for hi conduct the pre- vious evening. They regretted that his personal appear- ance had: to some extent been spoiled, and that they could not give him any pecuniary compensation, as they would have been only too glad to do so. Mr. Hunt also thanked Sergeant Stacey on behalf of the police.

The coroner's jury who inquired into the fatal mis- adventure of a woman parachutist at Peterborough on Tuesday added to their verdict of accidental death the following expression of opinion :—" That since no useful purpose is served by these senseless exhibitions, at which the lives of performers are risked, they should be made illegal."

THE IRON TRADE.

The iron trade is slowly recovering from the extreme depression whieh has prevailed for some time past, and now that the elections are over greater attention is being paid to business. But little improvement in the home trade, however, is expected owing to the unsatisfactory prospects of the harvests. Competition is still very keen in most departments, production being in excess of con- sumptive requirernvnts. So far as pig-iron is concerned, this is mainly due to the displacement of finished iron by steel, the raw material for which is largely imported from the North. There is a slight relaxation of Northern and Welsh competition in pig-iron, which is beneficially affecting makers in the Midlands, and prices are consequently slightly tending upwards. Even at present prices, however, smelters are not encouraged to increase the make, and some firms are indifferent about accepting orders at current rates except accompanied by specifi- cations. Northamptenshire and Derbyshire forge pig realized 395. to 404., and foundary qualities ss. to 75.6 d. extra. Staffordshire part-nine pig was quoted 395. and upwards, and foundry 45,. to 471.61. In the finished- iron department black sheets were in leading request, principally for galvanizing purynses, and prices were maintained at the recent advance, 24 gauge sheets realizing £6 17s. 63. to £7, and Isttens £7 ss. to £7 15s. Galvanized sheets were sold, delivered in Liverpool, at £lO3. sd. average for doubles. Marked bars were un- changed in value, but common bars were irregular, varying from £5 to £5 10s. at works, subject to the usual discount of 2i per cent. A moderate inquiry was experienced for steel, alike for bars, sheets, plates, and billets, and prices were steady at £4 10s. average for billets of basic quality.
