SOCIETY.

YOUNG BIRDS' RACE FROM WORCESTER. 84

mmrs.

This Focietv held its first race of the season for young birds on Thursday week, 15 members sending 231. birds for competition. The birds were despatched to the race point for liberation by the Manchester Flying Club's Con- voyer on Tuesday night. The race should have taken place on the following day, but owing to the inclement weather the race was held over until Thursday. The toss was effected at 9 a.m, in hazy weather but with a very favourable breeze blowing from the south. Mr. J. Longton, Bank-street, who has been so successful in the old birds' races this season was first again showing that his loft is in grand form. His first bird was timed In at 10.54 am, being 1 hour and 54 minutes in com- pleting the iburney

A DESTRUCTIVE WEAPON.

The latest invention of Mr. Hiram 2. Maxim is a portable machine gun that can be carried on a man's back, and set up for use upon a stand not altogether unlike a photographic equipment. The entire outfit weighs 45 pounds, and can he unpacked and fixed in 58 seconds. The muzzle, from which a steady stream of lead pours at the rate of 600 bullets a minute, can be manipulated like the nozzle of a garden-hose. It is claimed, moreover, that, owing to the stability of thc stand, the line of fire is more accurate than from any gun held to the shoulder. The new weapon is con- structed on the same principle as the Maxim quick. firing gun employed for military and naval purposes. It has a range of 3,200 yards, and sends a bullet at a velocity of 1,850 feet per second. A shot from it will penetrate 40 inches of oak. The new gun when ready for action resembles in some manner a brass telescope, with a pistol butt for a peep hole.

===== 0002601_18950810_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VALUE OF ADVERTISING.

A curious proof of the value of newspaper advertis- ing is cited by one of the largest advertisers in London. Be says :—" We once hit upon a novel expedient for ascertaining over what area our advertisements were read. We published a couple ot half-column ads.,' in which we purposely mis-stated half a dozen historical facts. In less than a week we received between 300 and 400 letters from all parts of the country from people wishing to know why on earth we kept such a consum- mate idiot who knew so little about English history. The letters kept pouring in for three or four weeks. It was one of the best paying • ads.' we ever printed ; but we did not repeat our experiment, because the one I refer to served its purpose. Our letters came from school- boys, girls, pr3fessors, clergymen, school teachers, and in two instances, fr im eminent men who have a wide wide reputation. I was more impressed with the value of advertising from those two advertisements than I should have been by volumes of theories."

===== 0002601_18950810_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
PAINTING HOUSES B/ MACHINERY.

It will be remembered (says "Chambers's Journal ") that the painting of the extensive buildings at the recent World's Fair at Chicago was executed with a machine, which, by means of compressed air, sprayed the colour on to any surface required, and altogether dispensed with the services of the ordinary paint-brmh. A compact machine for this work has recently been patented by a Manchester firm. The paint or tar is atomised and sprayed on the work with the help of an attached air pump, the nnzzle from which the liquid is projected taking the form of an injector. The machines are made in different sizes, and the smallest will cover three square yards of surface per minute. In a recent trial, a large girder with its connections was painted by this machine in two hours, representing an amount ef labour which it was calculated could not be done by a man and brush in a day.

WEEKLY REPORT ON THE HEALTH OF WARRINGTON. Week ending 27th July, 1895. During the week, which was the 30th of the current year, the following births and deaths were registered : Deaths. Births. Warrington 3B 5 Latchford 3 1

Total 4l

The excess of births over deaths was 35. The death- rate from all causes was equal to 54 per 1,000, against 11.3 the average of the previous four weeks, and against 10.2 the corresponding week of last year. Etttero to tin Otiitor.

_ AN ACKNOWLEDGMENT. Sir,—On Saturday, July 6th, my son George Thornley, was accidentally drowned whilst bathing in the Mersey, near Penketh. Only the day before he had signed a proposal in the presence ot Mr. Barr att, an agent, to assure with the Prudential Assurance Com- pany, Limited, for a sum of £9 17s. He had not paid anything, neitlaer was any benefit due, yet, the directors, of the Prudential very generously agreed to make me a present of the antount..and Mr. A. H. Harvey, one of their assistant superintendents,called a few days ago and I banded me the acceptable sum of £9 1.75. I therefore ask you to insert this as a public recognition of such kindness.—Yours very truly, JAMES THORNLEY. Sloop Fold, Great Sankey, Warrington, July 29th, 1895.

WARRINGTON.

THE WORKINGMEN'S GOSPEL TEMPERANCE SiISSION HALL: An enjoyable social evening was held in the above hall on Saturday. On Sunday night a well- attended gospel temperance meeting was held in the usual place at the end of Bewsey-street. Mr. John Drury presided. Earnest addresses were de- livered by tke Chairman, Mr. J. W. Tanner and Mr. J. Rigby. Ilifr.Henry Heald gave an interesting recitation entitled, " Moderation is Botheration." Two men signed the temperance pledge. DISPUTE OVER A. PIG. At the Warrington County Court on Thursday,be- fore his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, Sarah McCoy, Pierpoint-et., sued Frances Mellor, of Bridge-st., for El 13s. Id., for loss on the value of a pig alleged to have been purchased by Mrs. Mellor from plaintiff on July Ist. The plaintiff spoke to calling upon the defendant on July Ist. After bar- gaining, the latter agreed to buy a certain pig, and ordered plaintiff to kill it on the following Monday and send it to the shop on Tuesday. Defendant had further sent her son down to plaintiff's home, and he, on seeing the pig, also said it was to be sent on the Tuesday ready for sale. The pig was accord- ingly killed and sent on the following Tuesday,but when it arrived at the shop,the defendant said that she would throw it out if it was brought into the shop. Witness was consequently very excited as she did not know what to do with a dead pig. She had been to the bridewell, but of course could get no satisfaction tbere.—The defendant said that she arranged with the defendant to send a pig which would not weigh more than about six score. Instead of its being of this size, the pig was nearly eleven score. She had distinctly told tbe plaintiff that one of seven score would be too large altogether,and she would not accept it. After evidence had been given by both parties, his Honour said that from the testimony given, the defendant had bought a pig which should not be more than six score, and, therefore, she was quite right in refusing the pig sent by the plaintiff, if she chose to do so. He must accordingly give judgment for the defendant.

===== 0002601_18950810_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
POLICE.

-- TILORSDAY.—Before Mr. S. Chandley (chairman), and Lieu. Colonel Ridgway. TEMPORARY AUTHORITY.—Henry Hughes was granted temporary authority to sell at the Star and Garter, Friars Green, in lieu of Thomas Mullins. THEFT FROM THE MARKET.—Mary Ann Jennings, a respectably dressed woman, was charged with stealing from a stall in the market place at 11.30 on Wednesday morning, a pocket handkerchief and two dozen buttons, valued 5d., the property of Edwin John Gregory. The Chief Constable said that the complainant lived at 56, Horsemarket-street, and had a drapery stall iii the War- rington market. At about 11 30, Mrs. Gregory was talk- ing to Inspector Place when defendant was seen to pick up a white handkerchief and putit in herpocket, Sseiog , this, Inspector Place detained the defendant and handed her over to Inspector Cox, and in her basket were found the two dozen buttons. On the way to the po!ice.l station, the defendant said that the handkerchief was on the floor, and that she picked it up. She was a highly i respectable woman, and had previously borne a good character. Mrs. Gregory upon being called said thatshe saw the defendant take the handkerchief. She also I denied in cross-examination by Mr. A. Browne, who appeared for the defendant, that defendant had bought the buttons. Further evidence was given by Inspector Place and Inspector Cox, the latter of whom said that on the way to the police-station, the defendantadmitted picking up the handkerchief hut with no bad intention. —Mr. Browne said that this was one of those lamentable eases for which there was no accounting. The defendant had no reason far so acting, and none of her friends had any idea why she should have committed such an action. Evidence of respectability was given by Mr. Ashcroft. superintendent of an assurance society, and the bench decided to simply record a conviction against the defendant. BEGOING.—Joseph Robinson was ordered to leave the town within three hours for begging in Mersey-street, the previous afternoon. Detective Monaghan proved ' the case. DRUNK AND DISORDERLY.—MichaeI Craghan was fined ss. and costs for being drank and disorderly on School Brow the previous day. UNWHOLESOME FISH IN THE MARKET. John Mather, a fishmonger Mr St. Helens, was charged , with having in his possession 601bs. of conger eels, and 101Ibs. of cod fish, on July 2nd, which was unfit for hurman food. The Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle) prosecuted, and saidthat the proceedings were taken under Se?dion 117 of the Public Health Act, which left upon the defence the onus of proving that the fish was not intended for human food. On the 2nd July, a num- ber of fish belonging to defendant were found in pickle in the cellar of the Market Hall, by Inspector Norman. who was inspecting the cellars. The fish were unsound, And were condemned in the ordinary way, Dr. J. Guest Gornall,assistant medical officer,certifying them as being unfit for human food.—Mr. Riley, solicitor, St. Helens, said that the defendant had been in business for 30 years, and for the last 18 years he had regularly attended the Warrington market. Nothing previously had been brought against him. He did not deny that the fish was unfit for food, but it was not exposed for sale, and must have gone had since it waS put into pickle. The defend- ant was willing to pay all the costs of the case, but he hoped that the magistrates would not be severe consider- ing the previous good character of the defendant. Dr. J. Guest Gornall said that if the pickle was good when the fish was put into it, the fish ought to keep good.— The magistrates inflicted a penalty of 20s. and costs,and also allowed the doctor's fee.

BOWLING.

RED LION v. HORSE AND JOCKEY.

The return fixture between the Red Lion and the Horse and Jockey Clubs took place on Saturday last, the 3rd inst.' on the ground of the former at Stockton Heath. The heavy rain about three o'clock interfered with the commencement of the game at the usual time, and it was four o'clock before a start could he made. The ground was in very ggod condition considering the downpour, though somewhat heavy, and this circum- stance favoured the visiting team somewhat, a fact which was borne out during the progress of the match, as with about half the game over, the home team were several points behind their opponents. In the latter games, however, the homesters played for all they were worth, and eventually the Red Lion team ran out win- ners, after a hard fight, with a majority of nineteen points. At the Wagon Works and Messrs. k. and J. Vicars, however, the number of absentees was no large, that it was decided to close the works at noon, and this was done. On Monday afternoon the employes of Messrs. E. T. Evans, Earlestown,together with several friends, had a picnic to Frodsham. Leaving Earlestown in a couple of wagonettes, the party numbering about 40, had a very pleasant drive, and soon after arrival, tea was partaken of at Mersey View. GOOD TEMPLAIIS.

The quarterly meeting of the Hope of Earleatown Lodge of Good Templars was held in the Co-opera- tive Hall on Monday evening. Bro. SV. Wilson pre- sided, and there was a good attendance. One can- didate was admitted to membership and several others proposed. The digest of business for No. 1 Convention was read, and the representatives ap- pointed were Bros. J. Hatton, G. Clarke, and W. Wilson. The quarterly reports of the officers were then submitted and adopted. The Secretary (Bro. Barker) said that they had 145 members on the books, and the lodge was in a very good position. During the quarter several public meetings had been held, and visits had been paid to assist other lodges. The Financial Secretary (Bro. W. Williams) and Treasurer (Bro. T. Holliday) reported that the finances were satisfactory, and after clearing off all liabilities there was a good balance in hand. Bro. Newman, S.J.T., reported in connection with the juvenile temple, that meetings had been held re- gularly during the quarter, and everything was in a flourishing state. Bro. Quartermaster-Sergeant Roberts, of Warrington, arrived and was asked to preside and instal the officers for the ensuing quarter as follows :--C.T. Bro.J. Hatton, V.T. Sister A. Newman. Sec. B. Barker, F. Sec. E. Hodnett, Treasurer, T. Holiday; Chap. C. Drew; Marshall, G. E Warburton ; D.SI Sister E. Jones; guard, Bro. Willis ; Sen., G. Clarke. An address on temperance work in general was afterwards given by Bro. Roberts. and the thanks of the lodge were tendered to the past officers for their services, which closed an interesting session.

ALARMING TRAP ACCIDENT AT EARLESTOWN.

On Monday afternoon shortly after two o'clock, a trap accident of an alarming character happened in Legh-st., Earlestown. A young man named James Smith living in Sankey-sr. l‘tred a trap from Jr. S. Hazleton, and a horse from Mr. NicDermott, for the purpose of having an afternoon's outing. He had got, into the trap opposite is home, when from an unexplained cause the horse took fright, and set off at a terrific rate in the direction e,f Legh-st. Smith endeavoured to check its career, but the rein broke, and he thus lost all control of the animal. Turning the corner out of Sankey-r. into Legh-st., it rushed on the footpath between Mr. Potter's shop and the lamp post, the near wheel coming in contact with the lamp, which caused the horse to swerve to the left, and it dashed across the street, coming in con- tact with an empty shop on the corner. Here one of the shafts broke and the wheels were stripped from under, the trap turning completely over, with Smith underneath. The horse got loose, and stood quietly by gazing on the wreckage. As soon as possib!e, assistance was rendered, and Smith was taken from under the trap, when it was found that besides being badly shaken, he had received two seve-e Outs on the head, and his hands were lacer- ated. Ile was attended by Dr. Handyside, who dressed his wounds.

===== 0002601_18950810_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
DAHLIA QUEEN FESTIVAL AT BUETCNWOOD._

On Monday afternoon a new departure in the coronation of mimic "Queens " was made at, Bur- ton wood, in the coronation of a "Dahlia Queen." On each side, in neighbouring village Q they hare had their " \ fay "or " Rose Queen festivals,and the children of l3tirtonwood thought they could inauem- rate something differen', at any rate in name. Ac- cordingly a few weeks back a number of them waited upon Mr. George Anders, of the Bridge Inn. Burtonwood, and solicited his assistance. Ever ready to help the children in any scheme they may bring forward for their enjoyment, Mr. and Mrs. Anders entered heartily into their propositimis, and it was decided to have a "Dahlia' Queen. Mr. Anders undertook to provide the sinews of war, if the children performed their part. and selected their characters. After some deliberation th€y selected as the first " Dahlia Queen" Miss Florrie Horabin, 13 years of age, and Monday (Bank Holi- day) was fixed noon for the festival. The field be- hind the Bridge Inn was laid out for the occasion, a nicely decorated Throne being erected, directly op- posite being a May pole, or to preperly describe ii, a" Dahlia pole." The day was beautifully fine.and there was consequently a large gathering of specta- tors from Eariestown, Sutton, Warrington, &e. The whole arrangements were carried out by Mr. An- ders, who was assisted by Mr. J. H Anders, and the procession moved off the fi-ld about two o'clock, headed by the Sankey brass hand. The route Was through Burtonwood, Collins Green, to Bold oun- dry then back by way of the Old Lane to the Bridge Inn, which was reached abont 4.30. Several lurrie- end other conveyances were requisitioned, which owing to the length of the route were found most, convenient. The procession included the Queen, who was prettily attired in primrose-coloured sal in, with a train of ermine, mimic archbishop. sword bearer, flower girls, niggers, colliers, Red Riding Hoods, with dog to represent the wolf. floral dancers, white slave, Britannia, nurses, Swiss girls, bakers, Ally Sinner and wife, &c. On arrival at. the ground the Queen was conducted to the throne, and crowned byMi4s M A..lackson. of Bank House, Collins Green, who presented "Her MajestA" with a Bible. The children Were afterwards enter- tained to a substantial tea, the general catering be- ing carried out by Mr. Joseph Littler, of Earles- town. The subsequent proceedings included plaiting the ribbons, floral dances, and entertainment by the Earle.atown Nigger Troupe, all of which were much appreciated by the spectators.

THE EXAMINEE, SATURDAY, AUGUST 10, 1895

NEWTON-LE WILLOWS HORTICUL- TURAL SOCIETY.

ANNUAL SHOW.

The tenth annual exhibition of fruit, flowers and vege- tables held under the auspices pi the above Society, took place on Saturday atteruoon on the ground ot the Earlestowu Football Club, the Alesues. GAS AND WATER COMMITTEE.

This committee will submit the following minutes :

_ The carbonizing account for the past month was read —furl used 157 tons : gas made 1,592,300 feet, being an average of 10,110 feet per ton of fuel used. The Gas Manager's report and requirements were read and adopted. The illuminating quality of the gas varied from 19 to 20 candles. Thz, gas main in the St. Helens- road had been broken in two places owing to subsidence. Upwards of 200 automatic gas meters had now been fixed. The increased water consumption over the same month last year was 817,000 gallons. It was resolved, that the gas manager he requested to furnish an annual return of the arrears of accounts in his books ; also a description and value of old materials on the works at end of each financial year. The London and North-Western Company's account for water supplied for the six months ending May last, amounting to £707 33. 61., together with sundry other accounts were examined and passed to the Finance Com- mittee for payment.

A SHORT WAY WITH GAMBLERS.

In Ent,land when a young man ruins himself with eamhling. he becomes a nuisance and a disgrace to his , family and all his relatives. In the Malay Archipelago, they do not submit to this. They club him to death. This is what a writer in The NAM:VaI(' Weekly Chronicle" says the island of °nylon cock-fightine •s carried to a great height. The Sumatrans are addicttd ,to the use of dire. A strong spirit of play characterises a Malayan. After having resigned everything to the ,yond fortune of the winner, he is reduced to a ITOrrid 9ta,.P of desperatinn ; he then loosens a certain lock of ,ir. which indicates war and destruction to all whom the ravin;c qamester meets, lie intoxicates himself with orOnm ; and working himself into a fit of frenzy,he bites or kill everyone who comes in his way. Rut as soon as this lock is seen flowing. it is lawful to frsr at the person and to destroy him as fast as OnS• Ohl,. This custom is what is called "To run a-muck." To di:charge their gambling debts, the Siamese sell their possessions. their families, and at length them- selyee. the Chinese nlay niviat and day, till they have lost all they are worth ; and then they usually go and hone thernaclves. Such is the propensity of the Japanese for lih nl v. that they were compelled to make a law, that " Whoever ventures his money at play shall he put to death." In the newly-discovered islands of the Pacifi7 Ocean, tiler venture even their hatchets, which they bold as invaluable acqnisitiens, on running, matches'' We saw a man." says Cook, " beating his breast and tearing his hair in the violence of his rage,for haying. lost three hatchets at one of these races, and which he had purchased with nearly half his property.'

LOCAL VOLUNTEERS UNDER CANVAS'

The 2nd (Earl of Chester's) Volunteer Regiment of which the Runcorn Volunteers form a part, has since last Saturday, been having its annual experiences in camp. As was the case last year, the camp was pitched in a field near the South Shore, Blackpool. They forte a portion of the brigade camp which includes the Ist and 2nd Volunteer Battalion Royal Welsh Fusiliers, 2nd Shropshire, and the Ist Hereford. The preparatious for the convenience of the members of the corps were some- what interfered with through the unfavourable weather. During Friday night rain fell in torrents and was driven through the tents, thus rendering the con- dition of those in charge very uncomfortable. All was, however, in readiness for the reception of our local de- fenders who arrived on Saturday evening. The officers' lines were pitched in the rear, then came the staff sergeants' line, after which were the abodes of the serge, ants and men. The cyclists,who are under the command of Sergeant Hack, have three tents to themselves, as also have the signallers,under Sergeant Holland ,accommoda- tion being provided for. The six regiments comprise between 4,000 and 5,000 volunteers. In addition to the temporary habitations there are the usual departments for stores, for canteen purposes, and for the officers and sergeants in which to have their meals. The camp ground is well adapted for the purpose for which it is utilized, being level as well as sandy,the latter qualifica- tion making it porous, so that soon alter a shower it is comparatively dry. The respective companies comprising the 2nd Earl of Chester's Regiment stationed at Chester, Run- corn, Hartford, and Frodsharn, travelled by two special trains on Saturday, the first contingent from Chester and Frodsham reached the camp ground about seven o'clock, but the other portion did not arrive until about nine o'clock. They were headed by their respective bands. The strength of the battalion consists of 23 officers, 46 sergeants, 74 band and buglers, and 447 rank and file, or a total muster of about 600, which is considered satisfactory. Toe cfficers in attendance were—Colonel Ash- ton, Major Hazlehurst, Captain and Adjutant Turton, Hon. Majors T. Woolley, and G. W. Dutton, Dr. King (medical officer),the Rev. Dr. Preston (chaplain), Cap- tains J. Smith, H. Harrison, C. Gainon,H. J. Thompson, anu G. F. Ashton; Lieutenants F. 0. Evans, C. E. Bromley, E. L. Clarke, It. E. B. Brundrit, S. Thorneley, J. B. R.iyle, R. L. Sinclare Nichloson and Garrett, and Quartermaster C. Edwards. file onerous duties of sergeant-major are performed by Sergeant-Major J. Thomas, with the assistance of Sergeant-Instructors Evans, Burke and Callaghan. The orderly-room labours are executed by Quartermaster-Sergeant C. P. Jones and Sergeant-Instructor McGarvie. The stores are once more under the care of Quartermaster-Sergeants Parkin and Hankinson. The cooking arrangements are under the superintendence of Sergeant Hughes, of the Che- shire Regiment. The canteen has once more been en- trusted to Mr. George Shone, of the George and Dragon Hotel, Runcorn, and his efforts to provide fcr the wants of the men give every satisfaction. The catering for the officers' mess is in the hands of Messrs. Galt and Capper, of Liverpool, and the sergeants' mess is presided over by Sergeant Anderson. The brigade sergeant of police is Sergeant Harvey. On arrival at the camp, the men partook of refresh- ments, and shortly afterwards retired to rest. Their first night's experience was not however, of a very pleasant deserietion, for the rain which descended caused them no small amount of discomfort. The chaplain (Rev. Dr. Pieston) was one of the sufferers in consequ- ence of his luggage having got astray, and which had the result of causing him to take his lodgings on the ground. The next morning he found that his bedstead, which had come in very late, was out in the field. The Runcorn baggage also was left behind, and did not arrive until close upon midnight, and probably would not have reached even then but for the exertions of Cap- tain Ashton.

There was a large attendance of visitors from various parts whence the Volunteers had come, as well as from the immediate district at the usual service which was held on Sunday morning. The drums were erected, and the Union Jack. thrown across them. The Chaplain (the Rev. Dr. Preston) conducted the service.—Colonel Ash- ton, in dismissing the men for the day, said it was quite unnecessary for him to speak any words of advice to those who had previously been in camp, but he wished the recruits to observe military etiquette, and to salute the superior officers of the Brigade camp wherever they might meet them. He trusted their conduct would be of an exemplary character. He also urged them to use the utmost care if they went for a bathe. On Monday, the men set to their work in downright earnest. They were aroused at an early hour and during the day they put in three drills, namely, one at 8.30, another at 10.30, and the third at three o'clock. This was repeated on the following three days, and it is anti- cipated that as a result they will be in readiness to keep up the prestige of the regiment at the inspection, which takes place to-day (Friday) by Colonel Butlin. An in- teresting ceremony will follow this important event, for the Colonel will distribute the long service medals which have not yet been placed in the hands of those to whom they belong, namely, Lance-Corporal Blane, Corporal Rigby, Sergeant Carr, Corporal Roscoe, Quartermaster Brown, Colour-Sergeants Carr, A. Davies, and T. Davies, Private Roberts, and Corporal Simcock. The conduct of the men has been excellent, the only unpleasant in- cident being, that the Colonel has been obliged to hold a Court Martial over one member who was insubordinate at the drill at Runrorn, last Saturday week. The offender was dismissed from the regiment. The weather during the week has been showery. A correspondent writing on Wednesday save :—The battalions of the 2nd Earl of Chester's,lst and 2nd Royal Welsh Fusiliers, 2nd Shropshire, and let Hereford, form- ing the Welsh Border Brigade are experiencing very favourable weather, especially Brigade, drill purposes, at the camp. South Shore, Blackpool, so that the inspection by Colonel Butlin promises to re attended with success. Yesterday the members, numbering between 4,000 and 5,000, had a review under Brigade-General Carey, in the presence of a large number of spectators. The various movements were admirably gone through. On Tuesday they had a sham fight, four battalions being pitted against the 2nd Cheshire and let Shropshire, the latter of whom obtained an impregnable position, and were highly cnmplimented by the umpire,General Carey. The weather throughout each day has been fine, so that not a single drill has heen missed, thus enabling the men to gain efficiency. Each night, however, heavy showers have fallen, but without serious consequences or undue unpleasantness. 'rhere is very little sickness, and the conduct of the yoluntee-s has been of an exemplary character.

THE ROMANTIC ELOPEMENT FROM

WI INES. POLICE PROCEEDINGS.

On Thursday. at the Widnes Petty Sessions, before Messrs. J. Brock. D. M'Kechnie, and J. Mercer, William Rutherford, whose address was given as 43, Titchfield- st., Liverpool, was brought up on remand charged with steeling various articles of household furniture, the pro- perty of Nicholas Farrell, a chemical labourer, of 40, Wareing-st., Widnes, on the 30th tilt, It may be explained that the goods were removed on the evening of the date named on a lurry to the house in Liverpool, which had been previously rented by the pri- soner, who was there joined by Mrs. Farrell. The prose- cutor was at his work at the time the goods were re- moved, and the woman left her children at home. Far- rell took out a warrant on Thursday last, and on Friday the prisoner was arrested at 43, Titchfield-st. by ?oboe- constsble Worsford, He was remanded on Saturday, and, notwithstanding Mrs. Farrell's efforts on his behalf, he was unable to obtain sureties, and has been in custody since. " " ' • ' ' '

Nichols. Farrell, the prosecutor, stated that the pri- soner had lodged with him for about two years, up to the 30th ult. On the afternoon of that date, about half- past one. witness went to his work, leaving prisoner and witness's wife in the house. Witness returned in the evening, about ten o'clock, and found that nearly all the furniture was gone, as well as his wife and the lodger and two children. The goods he rnissed were worth more than £l5. and among them was a family Bible.— Cross-examined by the prisoner: Some of the goods he had obtained on the hire system, but he had already paid more than 15 for them. He had told his wife to go, hot he never told him (the prisoner) to take her. (Laughter.) His wife had said she wanted to an, and witness had said he had better go. He had never told his wife she could take the furni- ture.—Edwin Clav, carter in the employ of Mr. Sefton, coal dealer, said the prisoner met him in Loasdale-road about nine o'clock on the morning ,of the 30th ult. and arranged with witness to remove the furniture ?rem 40, Wareing-street to Liverpool at night. He wrote the name " Farrell. 40, Wareing-st.," on a leaf of his nock&-book and u'ave it him. The price arranged was 12s. Witness rook a lorry to the house about nine o'clock At night. Prisoner carried the goods to the lurry, and witness loaded them. He saw nothing of Mrs. Farrell until he arrived At 43, Titchfield-street, Liverpool, at two o'clock the next morning. Prisoner gave witness the 124., but he got it fr mu Mrs. Farrell. While removing the goods in Wareing- street a crowd of women and children con- gregated near the house, and the prisoner told witness that if anyone asked him where he was taking the furniture to say, "To West Bank," and witness did so when some young men asked him. The prisoner accomnanied the furry all the way to Liverpool with three children.—Police-constable Worsfold proved serv- ing the warrant upon the prisoner at 43,Titchfield-street Liverpool, on Friday afternoon. In reply to the charge, the prisoner said, " I never stole them. I am a lodger. She (pointing to Mrs. Farrell) said she would come away, and I said I would open barber's shop in. Liverpool." Both prisoner and Mrs. Farrell were in the house. Witness found the rent. book of the house in Titchfield-st.., which was in the prisoner's name.—After being cautioned, the prisoner made a long statement to the effect that on the 22nd tilt Farrell told his wife to go and take him (the pri- soner) with her,and that whatever she took he Would not fellow her across the street. It was Mrs. Farrell's stw- erstion that they should move to Liverpool and he did to. He had nothing whatever to do with the things. He briieved they were Mrs. Farrell's own property, as he had heard her and her husband arguing the point.—The prlsoner was committed for trial at the County Quarter Sc Wong.

CRICKET.

SANBEY WIRE MILLS V. laLem.—This match was played at Irlam on Bank Holiday, before a fair number of spectators. As this was the initial appearance of the wire men at Irlam a considerable amount of interest was centred ia the encounter, especially as the homesters have not for some seasons tasted defeat on their own turf .The home team won the:toss,and decided to bat but made poor use of their opportunities, the whole team being out for the poor total of 39, a result due to the really splendid fielding and bowling of the visitors. The visitors' first two bats opened very quietly, and small as the home score was the game was anybody's up to the very last. When the last man came in 4 runs were I wanted to tie, but playing carefully run by run was got till Evans by a splendid drive clean out of ground for 4, the match. The innings eventually closed for a total of 45. The home team batted the 2nd inn- ings, and were again dismissed for a poor total 47. The visitors losing one wicket for 14, when time saved the home team from a heavier defeat. Scores :

FRODSHAIf.

SUSPECTED OUTBREAK OF SWINE FEYER.—OH Sunday it was found that two pigs belonging on premises at Overton, were suffering from some disease, and on Tues- day, a pig was found dead at a place in Frodsham. It is suspected that these are cases of swine fever, and they have been reported to the Board of Agriculture. THEFT OF WEARING APPAREL &C.—At Frodsham Police Court on Thursday morning before Mr. Thomas and Dr. Steele, Elizabeth Hoose, a widow, Overton, was charged with stealing a quantity of clothing, the property of Thomas Jones Overton, on the 30th July, and was further charged with stealing several articles of clothing and a tablecloth, the property of Mary Jefferies. The articles,were valued at 12s. 9d.—Mrs. Jones stated that the prisoner had been accustomed to work for her at times. She missed the articles and suspected the prisoner of stealing them. On charging the woman with the offence, she admitted it and said she would return the articles if no action was taken. She brought them back on the morning of the 3rd inst.—Mrs. Jefferies gave evidence as to the prisoner having had occasion to visit her house. She missed thearticles in question and found that the prisoner had taken them.—Elizabeth Walker and Ellen Jannion spoke to the prisoner having offered them some of the articles for sale. She told thema that she wanted the money for bread.—P.C. Wetherby appre- hended the prisoner the previous day. He charged her with the theft and she admitted it. —She appealed to the magistrates to be lenient with her and said it walall through drink. She promised to reform, and the magis- trates decided to fine her is. and 10s. costs. MARRIAGE OF THE REV. CHARLES STEELE.—A marri- age in which a great deal of local interest was taken, was celebrated at St. Albans' Church, Tattenhall, on Wednesday afternoon, when Miss Violet Causer Lowe, elder daughter of the late Mr. William Lowe, of Liver- pool, and the Rev. Cnarles Steele, son of Dr. Steele, of Netherton, Fradsham, and formerly of the Old Quay works, Runcorn, were united in the bonds of holy matrimony. The officiating clergyman was the Rev. Canon Holme, who was assisted by the Rev. Ffoliott Sandford, Vicar of Sharrow, Sheffield, and the Rev. Charles Arnold, Vicar of Tattenball. The service was fully choral, and the proceedings were witnessed by a good number of spectators. The bride was prettily attired in a dress of white silk, trimmed with lace. She wore a wreath of orange blossoms, and carried a beauti- ful bouquet. She was accons;,anied by two bridesmaids, Miss Lowe (sister of the bride) and Miss Steele (sister of the bridegroom). They were attired in dresses of white muslin, trimmed with pink corded silk, and wore hats, with flowers to match. They were each I adorned with pearl brooches, the gift of the bridegroom, and carried bouquets of pink carnations. The bride was given away by her uncle, Mr. Corbet Lowe. Two pages, the sons of the Rev. Charles Arnold, were also in attendance, and were dressed in suits of Henry VIII. period. The best man was Mr. M. Steele, jun., brother of the bridegroom. As the party left the church, Men- delssohn's wed-known " Wedding March "was played. An " At Home " was afterwards held at the vicarage, kindly placed at the party's disposal by the Vicar. About 100 guests were present. The happy couple departed later in the afternoon for Grange en route for the Eng- lish lakes. The bride wore a travelling dress of dark green with whita silk waistcoat trimmed with lace. The presents, numbering about 200, were of a costly descrip- tion.

MASSACRE OF ENGLISH WOMEN IN

CHINA.

HORRIBLE ATROCITIES.

A horrible massacre of five lady missionaries of Eng- lish nationalty has taken place at Ku-Cheng, a perfec- ural capital in the Province of lionan, situated a hundred miles east of Foochow, on the Yellow River. The ladies were attacked and butchered by the natives after unspeakable atrocities had been committed upon them. Other foreigners, amongst whom were several women and children, were more or less seriously wounded on the same occasion. The Chinese officials again connived at, if they did not instigate, the outrage. The foreign community at Shanghai look upon the com- mission sent 'to Cheng-tu to inquire into the recent riots as a farce, the more so as several officials who were openly charged with complicity have been promoted instead of receiving punishment or censure. The Shanghai correspondent of the "Times," writing on Saturday, says :—The missionary sanatcrium at Whasang, near Ku-Cheng, in the province of Fokien, has been attacked. Five foreign ladies were killed. Two foreign ladies and two children were wounded and others are missing. . _ _

Telegraphing on Sunday, the same correspondent says :—Ten British subjects were killed at Ku-Cheng. The Rev. It. W. Stewart, Mrs. Stewart, and one child were burnt in the house. Misses Yellow, Mrs. Hall Saunders (2). Gordon (2), and Bessie Newcombe were murdered wits spears and swords. Miss Codrington was seriously wounded on the head. Mr. Stewart's eldest child had a kneecap badly injirei. The youngest child had an eve gouged out. The Rev. Mr. Philips, Dr. Gregory, and Miss Hartford escaped and have arrived safely at Fu-Chau-Fu. The last two, who are Ameri- cans, are wounded. The Cheng-Tu Commission of In- quiry includes the Prefect, who himself is seriously implicated.

PENKETH.

PARISH COUNCIL MEETING.—A meeting of this Coun• cil was held on Tuesday evening at the Day Star Techni- cal Institute, the Chairman (Mr. J. C. Routledge) pre- siding. There were also present Mr. C. E. Parker (vice- chairman), Dr. Murray, Mr. James Millington' Mr. Evan Cross, and Mr. Jonas Forster (clerk).—With regard to ' the Recreation ground, Mr. Parker said that, as the Council knew, Mr. Forster had received a letter from Mr. Campbell with reference to it. He (the speaker) wrote to Mr. Campbell in reply, but he did not seem to realise what had been done to the ground, and that they had spent £4O upon it in levelling it and improving the hedges. He had since had a long talk with him, and suggested that the best course to adopt was for Mr. Hughes to sell to them (if the Council could raise the money) his rights outright.— He thought further, as a Parish Council or privately, someone would have come forward and bought out the rights and have given the ground to the village as a whole. Mr. Campbell would not entertain that for a moment, but he would be willing to accept a nominal rent. He (Mr. Parker) suggested Se., but the reply was that that amount was not worth collecting. He men- tioned £2 or £l. He asked what would be the result if the Council felt inclined to improve the recreation ground by laying it out, and he re-asserted his right to the grazing. He said that could be arranged in the form of a lease, but he did not suggest any term.—Several questions having been answered by the Clerk as to the Powers of the Council.—Mr. Cross proposed that the Chairman and Vice-Chairman negotiate with Mr. Camp- bell for a settlement of the matter OR the lines referred to by Mr. Parker.—Mr. Millington seconded the motion, which was agreed to.—The Clerk submitted a map of the township, which it is intended to hang on one of the walls. It was examined by the members.—This was all the business of public intermit before the Council.

A resolution proposed by Alderman M`Kerrow in the Salford Council yesterday to memorialise the Govern ment to introduce a Bill giving local authorities power to license racecourses, so as to mitigate the evils of gambling was defeated by a large majority.

REE TO MEN. When you are tired of taking nauseous and mysterious prescriptions and compounds, which do you no good, but only ruin your digestive organs, write to me and I will send you a book which describes a QUICK AND CERTAIN care tor lost strength, wasting weakness, erc., from whatever causearising. NO STOMACH MEDICINE, NO ELECTRIC- ITY. Sent in envolope on receipt of two stamps for postage A. J. Luton. 92-93. Great Russell-street. London. W.C. A BOOK FOR MARRIED WOMEN. The information contained In this book ought to be known by every married woman, aud it will not harm the unmarriep to read. The book is cmveniently divided into 12 !chapters The first chapter treats of the changes at puberty, or when a girl becomes a woman. The second chapter treats of marriage from a doctor's standpoint ; points out the best age for marriage, and who should have children and who not, and furnishes useful information that one can ordinarily get only from an intelligent doctor. The third chapter treats of the marriage of blood relations; and condemns such marriages as a rule. Chapter four treats of the signs of pregnancy. The filth chapter tells how a woman should live during the pregnant state. The sixth chapter treats of mishaps and how to avoid them. The seventh chapter treats of maternal impressionts and shows that birthmarks are not due longings in the part of the mother, but rather to her poor health. The eighth chapter teaches how to have easy confinements. Certain people believe that women should bring forth in pain and trouble, but the hygienic physician says that confinements can be made comparatively easy i certain rules are obeyed ; these rules are given. The ninth chapter treats of the proper management of confinement until the baby is born. The tenth chapter tells how to treat the mother till she is up and about again. The eleventh chapter treats of sterility ; gives the main causes of it, how these may be overcome and children result The last chapter treats of the , "change," a most important article for all women over forty. The book is full of useful information and no book is written which goes so thoroughly into matters relating to married women. Some may think too much is told ; such can scarcely be the case, for knowledge is power and the means of attaining happiness. The book can be ha from Dr. T. R. ALLINSON, 25 Box. 4, Spanish-place. Manchester-square, London, W., in return for a Postal Order for is. Id.

WEEKLY CHAT.—Subject Second-hand Furniture.— The opening of Spring is the time for the busy house- wife to put her house in order, therefore the matter of furni- shing must be well discussed. We cannot, therefore, do better than advise a visit to Worsley's before definitely de- ciding where and what to purchase. This visit cannot pos- sibly but prove remunerative, if only in experience. " The Pantheon " being a hive of industry, it is a palatial pile of buildings close to the Central Station. having a frontage of nearly 150 feet This immense building is filled from floor to ceiling (six storeys) w ith the grandest collection of High- class Second-hand Furniture in the World. This is not an exaggeration, but can be verified by ocular siemonetration if you pay a visit to Manchester. Suites of Bedroom Furni- ture, for instance, by such makers as Goodall. (-lAUTluN.—Beware of buying new London jerrybuilt fur- niture, no matter how low the price is- it is dear at any price. Worstey's have second-hand reliable furniture by good makers, which will last for ages. Thore is no disputing the fact that high-class second-band funiture is better than new. The only fault is that sometimes the article is old- fashioned. This is easily remedied at Wm sley's, they having over 50 cabinet makers at work modernising second-hand fur- niture when necessary. For instance, second-hand side- boards are easily modernised by having the backs raised, the plates bevelled, and the panels carved. A SOLDIER'S HEROISM. In the Reminiscences of Gen. Sir Evelyn Wood, him- self a brave English soldier, a touching instance of courage and self-sacrifice is given. One June day in 1855 a detachment of English marines were the crossing the Woronzow road under fire from the Russian batteries. All of the men reached shelter in the trenches except a seaman, John Blewitt. As he was running a terrific roar was beard. His mates knew the voice of a huge cannon the terror of the army, and yelled," Look out ! It is Whistling Dick !" But at the moment Blewitt was struck by the enormous mass of iron on the knees, and was thrown to the ground. He called to his especia chum, "Oh, Welch ! save me !'"The fuse was hissing, but Stephen Welch ran out of the trenches, and seizing the great shell, tried to roll it off his comrade. It ex- ploded with such terrific force that not an atom of the bodies of Blewitt or Welch was found. GREAT MASSACRES. The following which has been taken from Ilaydns' "Dictionary of Dates," are among the most remarkable massacres since the time of Christ At the destruction of Jerusalem in the year 70, 1,100,000 Jews are said to have been put to the sword. Cassius, a Roman General under the Emperor Marcus Aurelius, put to death in the year 165, 300,009 of the in- habitants of Seleucia. At Alexandria in 215, many thonsanda of citizens were massacred by order of Antoninus. The Emperor Probus is said to have put to death 400,000 of the barbarian invaders of Gaul in 277. At Thessalonica in 390, 7,000 persons invited into the circus were put to the sword, by the order of Theo- dosius. A massacre of the Danes took place in the southern counties of Eogland, in the night of Nov. 13, 1002, by order of Ethelred 11. At London it was most bloody,the church being no sanctury. In the massacre af the Albigenses and Waldenses in 1208, commenced at Toulouse, thousands perished by the sword and gibbet. , tt Sicilian Vespers" is the term given to the massacre of the French which commenced on March 30, 1282, at Palermo. The conspirators assembled on Easter Mon- day, and while the French (who had conquered Sicily in 1266) were engaged in festivities, a Sicilian bride passed by with her train. One Drochet, a Frenchman, used her rudely, under pretence of searching for arms. A young Sicilian stabbed him with his own sword ; and a tumult ensued instantly. The populace ran through the city, crying out" Let the French die," and, without distinction of rank, age, or sex, slaughtered all of that nation they could find to the number of about 8,000. Even the churches proved no sanctuary. The massacre of St. Bartholomew commenced at Paris on the night of the festival, August 24, 1572. According to Sully, 78,000 Hugenots, or French Protestants, in- cluding women and children,w ere murdered throughout , the kingdom by secret orders from Charles IX. at the in- , stigation of his mother, the queen dowager, Catherine de Medici,. In 1592, 65,000 Christians in Croatia were slain by the Turks. In O'Neill's rebellion in Ireland a massacre of the Pro- ' teetants broke out in October 23,1641. Before the rebel- • lion was entirely suppressed the number slain was, according to Sir W. Temple, 154,000. In the massacre of Glencoe about 60 men of the clan Macdonald were slain for not surrendering before Janu- ary 1, 1692. Sir John Dalrymple, afterwards Earl of Stair, their enemy, obtained a decree, "to extirpate that set of thieves," which the king is said to have signed withom perusing. Every man under 70 was slain. This mandate was treacherously executed by 120 soldiers of a Campbell regiment, hospitably received by the High- landers, February 13, 1692. Many women and children, turned out naked in a freezing night, perished. At Batavia 12,000 Chinese were massacred by the natives, October, 1740, under the pretext of an intended Insurrection. After a long siege by the Russians, who lost 20,000 men before the place, the town of Ismail was taken by storm, December 22, 1790; when Suwarrow, the most merciless warrior of modern times, put the brave Turk- ish garrison (30.000 men) to the sword,and delivered oir the town to pillage, and ordered the massacre of 6,000 women. In the French Revolution a dreadful massacre took place in Paris, September 2-5, 1792. Tire prisons were broken open, and the prisoners butchered, among them an ex-bishop, and nearly 100 non-juring priests. Some accounts state the number of persons slain at 1,200, others at 4,000. In 1798, the insurgent Irish burnt, shot, or pierced to death by pikes, 184 men, women, and children, chiefly Protestants, at the barn of Scullabogue, Ireland. On March 1, 1811, the Mameluses were decoyed into the power of the Turkish Pach,s, Mehemet Ali, and slain at Cairo. On April 11, 1822, a dreadful massacre took place in the island of Chios (now Scio). When it was taken by the Turks during the Greek Insurrection they slew 40,000 of its inhabitants. On June 18, 1845, above 500 Kabyles, at war with the French, were suffocated in a cave, a fire having been ' kindled by order of General Pelissier, afterwards Duke of Malakoff. An insurrection, promoted by foreign agitators, broke out in Bulgaria in May,`lB76. It was quickly suppressed villages were bunnt by the Bashi- Bbyaztorookosp,s' S onie and ether 65 Turkish troops, and about 15,000 persons were killed. A CURIOUS COMPUTATION. -- President Andrews, of Brown University, contributed an article to the t. North American Review," entitled "Are There Too Many of Us ? " in which he made the following calculati.n :—The entire globe measures about 600,000,000,000,000 square yards, or, allowing a yard as standing room for four persons, there is a place for 2.400,000,000,000,000. Now the population of England and Wales, which may be regarded as about normal for civilised lands, doubled between 1801 and 1851. At this rate the population would in 100 years multiply itself by 4; in 200 by 18; in 1,000 tiv 1,000,000; and in 3 000 sears by 1.000,000,000,000,000,000. SJ that, even if we begin with a single pair, toe increase would in 3,000 years have become two qointulem human beings ; viz., to every square yard 3,333* persons instead of 4. Or the earth sveuiil be covered wok) men in columns of 833* each, stanciiug on each other's heads. If they averageu five feet tall, each column world be 4,1661 teet high."
